- **Free times:** the page's weekly hours cut into appointments of its duration (the same `freeSlots` as the website's calls, generalised with a time zone), after the notice and within the horizon, minus the owner's confirmed appointments on **any** of their pages (one person can't be in two places).
- **Booking:** public, 5/min/IP, with a honeypot (and the web treats a form filled in under 3 s as one: a fake success, nothing stored). Inside a transaction with a per-owner advisory lock, the time must still be free; otherwise `409`. Both sides get an email with an iCalendar invitation (`METHOD:REQUEST`); the visitor's has a cancel link `citas.codeqlick.com/{locale}/cancelar?token=…` (32 random bytes, stored as its SHA-256).
- **Reminder:** about 24 h before each confirmed appointment, whoever booked gets a reminder email in their language (when, where, the video link, reply-to the owner). It can't carry the cancel link (only its hash is stored), so it points to the confirmation email's link and the booking page. `CitasRemindersService` runs every 15 minutes in the API process (off in tests) and sends for appointments starting in 2–24 h, skipping those booked less than 26 h ahead (their confirmation is fresh). Each reminder is claimed by setting `Appointment.reminderSentAt` before sending, so it goes out once.
- **Cancelling:** the visitor from that link (shows the appointment first; past appointments can't be cancelled), or the owner from their list. Either way both sides are emailed with `METHOD:CANCEL` and a higher `SEQUENCE`. Deleting a page cancels its upcoming appointments first.
- **Privacy:** pages are `noindex`; the owner sees their visitors' name, email, phone and note; the account export (`/auth/me/export`) includes the person's pages and their appointments.

### 4.10 Swipe (`modules/swipe`)

Codeqlick's fourth own web app, at **swipe.codeqlick.com**: adults share intimate photos of themselves, others swipe them (right likes, left skips), and two people who liked a photo of each other match and can chat. Spec in the Notion roadmap row "Swipe 18+". Decision 27. It is listed on the site's `/apps` page with an 18+ warning (owner's request, 2026-11-08); since 2026-11-09 (owner's request) its header and its favicon (the site's, which Google shows in results) carry the Codeqlick logo, and its public pages (`/`, `/rules`, `/takedown`) are indexed like the video chat's: canonical and hreflang on its own host, Open Graph image at `/{locale}/og`, `rating: adult` so SafeSearch filters it, its own sitemap and a robots.txt that keeps out `/deck`, `/photos`, `/matches`, the images and the sign-in pages, which also send `noindex`.

- **Entering:** signed-in accounts only. Like the video chat: date of birth checked ≥ 18 and **not stored** (only its year, since round 2), plus the rules (`SWIPE_TERMS_VERSION`; a new version asks again). No identity document: the owner chose declared age (2026-11-08), so the safeguards below rest on the team's review.
- **Verification photo before uploading:** to upload, a person first sends a photo of themselves holding a paper with their nickname, "Swipe" and the date. The team approves or rejects it (`/admin/swipe`); only the team sees it, and a rejected one is deleted. Moderators reject anyone who doesn't clearly look adult.
- **Verified badge and your week (owner's request 2026-11-09):** cards, the matches list and the chat header show a fuchsia tick next to a nickname whose verification photo was approved (`verified` on cards and match partners; since only verified people can upload, every card has it). `/photos` starts with the person's week once they can upload (`GET /swipe/stats`): views (any vote on their photos), likes, «me encanta» and new matches, for the last 7 days and the 7 before (index `swipe_votes(owner_id, created_at)`).
- **Photos:** up to 10 active per person, 10 uploads a day, JPEG/PNG/WebP up to 10 MB with explicit consent ("only I appear, I am 18+, I took it or let it be taken, I want it here"). The browser shrinks them first (≤ 1600 px, JPEG ≤ 900 KB, because the apps' Nginx sites take 1 MB). The API re-encodes with sharp (rotation applied, ≤ 1600 px, WebP, **metadata stripped**: no camera data or location), computes a 64-bit dHash and refuses images within 6 bits of a blocked hash. Files live in `SWIPE_UPLOADS_DIR` as `<uuid>.webp` (mode 0600); a cleanup every 6 hours deletes files no row points to (deleted photos and accounts). Every photo starts **PENDING** and only the team publishes it (LIVE), comparing it with the verification photo.
- **Seeing photos:** `GET /swipe/photos/:id/file` streams one to its owner, an admin, or a person who can see it in the deck (LIVE, not blocked either way, owner not suspended), otherwise `404`. The web proxies it (`/{locale}/swipe/img/<id>`) with `Cache-Control: private`. Cards show the nickname, gender, interests, the photo's ref and a watermark overlay (nickname · swipe.codeqlick.com); images can't be dragged or long-pressed.
- **Deck and matches:** 10 LIVE photos at a time, never my own, already voted, blocked or from a suspended owner, filtered by whom I want to see (`seeking`). A like counts on the photo; when the owner has also liked one of my photos, a match is created (once) and returned with the vote. A match's chat is text only (≤ 1000 characters, 20/min), the web polls every 4 s; ending a match deletes its chat, blocking also hides both people from each other.
- **Reports:** from a card or a chat (MINOR · NON_CONSENSUAL · STOLEN · ILLEGAL · HARASSMENT · SPAM · OTHER, 20 a day). MINOR, NON_CONSENSUAL and STOLEN **hide the photo at once** (HIDDEN) until reviewed, and MINOR emails the team. From a chat the last 30 messages are saved with the report. The team dismisses (a hidden photo goes back live if nothing else is open) or acts: remove the photo, block its image, suspend the person (24 h, 7 d, 30 d or permanent).
- **Removal requests (DSA):** a public form at `/{locale}/swipe/takedown`, no account needed (5/min/IP, honeypot): email, the photo's ref (optional), reason and details. A matching ref hides the photo at once; the team is emailed and the person gets an acknowledgement. Answered from `/admin/swipe` within 24 hours: remove (deletes the photo and blocks its image) or close (restores it); the person is emailed the answer in their language.
- **Privacy:** the date of birth isn't stored (only its year, for the age filter); files only reach browsers through the access checks; the account export includes the Swipe profile, photos (refs and statuses), likes given, messages sent and reports made; deleting the account deletes everything (files within 6 hours).
- **Improvements, round 2 (owner's request, 2026-11-08):**
  - **Emails to people** (in their account's language, never with a photo, a nickname or message text, so a glance at the inbox gives nothing away): verification approved or rejected (with the team's note), photo published or rejected (with the reason), a new match (to the person who didn't make it), a «me encanta» on one of my photos, and unread messages: a check every 10 minutes emails once per match when the partner's messages have waited 15 minutes unread, and not again until I read them (`SwipeMatch.notifiedAAt/BAt`). A profile switch turns off the match, «me encanta» and message emails (`emailAlerts`, on by default); the team's decisions are always mailed (the reason for a removal is owed under the DSA).
  - **Team digest:** every 30 minutes, when something new is waiting (verifications, photos, open reports, removal requests) since the last digest, `LEADS_NOTIFY_TO` gets one email with the counts and the link to `/admin/swipe`, at most once an hour. MINOR reports and removal requests still email at once.
  - **Own legal texts:** `/{locale}/swipe/terms-of-use` and `/{locale}/swipe/privacy-policy` (owner from `LEGAL_OWNER_*`, like the site's). The privacy policy names the special category data (sex life, GDPR art. 9.2.a: explicit consent at upload), what is kept and for how long, OVHcloud as the only processor, and the rights; both are linked from the entry screen, the footer and the rules. `SWIPE_TERMS_VERSION` moves to `2026-11-08.2`, so everyone accepts them on their next visit (which also stores the birth year of people who entered before).
  - **Phone app and notifications:** on the Swipe host the manifest installs "Swipe" (its own icon, no Codeqlick logo) and `/swipe-sw.js` shows Web Push notifications for a new match, a new message and a «me encanta», with the same VAPID keys as the video chat (off without them). Subscriptions in `SwipePushSubscription` (`POST/DELETE /swipe/push`); the profile has the switch.
  - **Filters:** besides whom I want to see (`seeking`), an age range (18–99) and «only my country». The age comes from the **birth year only** (`birthYear`, stored from the date of birth on entry; people who entered before set it in their profile); people without it only appear when the range is the full one. The country is optional (`country`, ISO code from a fixed list).
  - **Richer profile:** a sentence about me (`bio`, ≤ 160 characters) shown on cards and chats, with the interests that already exist.
  - **Undo and «me encanta»:** `POST /swipe/votes/undo` undoes my last vote when it is under 10 minutes old and didn't make a match (a like is subtracted). A vote can be a «me encanta» (`SwipeVote.superLike`, 3 a day): the owner is told (email and push) and the photos of whoever loved one of theirs come first in their deck, marked.
  - **Watermark in the file:** photos are stored with the nickname, the photo's ref and swipe.codeqlick.com burnt into a corner (the hash is taken before, so blocking still works). Photos stored before are marked once by the background task (`SwipePhoto.watermarked`).
  - **Safer chat:** until each person has sent 5 messages in a match, a message with a phone number, an email, a link or an outside app or handle (WhatsApp, Telegram, Instagram, Snapchat, OnlyFans, @name…) is refused with an explanation (scams and spam). Ending a match stays in the chat's menu.
  - **Blurred photos:** a profile switch (`blurPhotos`) shows the deck's and the chats' photos blurred until tapped.
- **Private album (owner's choice 2026-11-09):** besides the 10 public photos, up to 5 photos in a private album (`SwipePhoto.inAlbum`). They are reviewed like any photo, never appear in the deck (and can't be voted), and only people matched with the owner see them: the chat shows a closed «Álbum privado de …» with the thumbnails (blurred when the viewer chose blurred photos), the matches list shows how many there are (`partner.albumPhotos`), and the file route lets a viewer through only with a match. A photo moves into or out of the album from «Mis fotos» without a new review; reports, takedowns and admin tools work on album photos as on any other (the admin lists tag them).
- **Ads (ExoClick, like the video chat, 4.7):** the same 300x250 banner tag, from a separate ExoClick site for swipe.codeqlick.com with its own verification tag. Two zones (`SWIPE_AD_ZONES` in `lib/videochat-ads.ts`): `deck` under the deck, `pages` at the bottom of the landing and of the matches list; never inside a chat or over a photo. `AdZone` takes `app="swipe"`; a zone without an ID renders nothing, so the release that fills the IDs turns them on. Swipe's host gets the same CSP as the video chat's (ExoClick's hosts in `script-src`, `connect-src`, `frame-src`).

### 4.11 Errors log (`modules/errors`)

Owner's choice (2026-11-09): know about a bug before a person writes in, without an outside service. Unexpected errors from three places are grouped in `ErrorGroup` and shown in Admin → Errors:

- **API:** `ProblemDetailsFilter` records every error that is not an `HttpException` (the ones that become a generic 500), with the request's method, path, request id and user agent.
- **Website server:** `src/instrumentation.ts` (`onRequestError`) posts every error Next.js reports while rendering, in a Server Action or a route handler to `POST /errors` (`source: server`, with the digest the visitor's error page shows). Production only; an `ApiError` with a 4xx (the visitor's doing) or 503 (the API down, which the server monitor alerts on) is left out.
- **Visitors' browsers:** `components/layout/ErrorReporter` (in the root layout) listens for uncaught errors and promise rejections, and the error pages (`[locale]/error.tsx`, `(site)/error.tsx`) report what they catch. Errors with a digest came from the server and are skipped; so is noise nobody can fix (`lib/error-report.ts`: extensions, cross-origin scripts such as the ads, network failures, chunks of an older release). At most 5 reports per page load, through a Server Action (the BFF) that forwards the visitor's IP. Production only.

**Grouping:** the fingerprint is the SHA-256 of the source, the message without ids, numbers, URLs and quoted values, and the first stack frame of the app's own code without line numbers or bundle hashes, so the same bug stays one group across occurrences and releases. A repeat adds to `count` and updates the last occurrence's details.

**Alerts:** a new group, or a resolved one that happens again (it opens again), emails the team inbox (`LEADS_NOTIFY_TO`, English) with the message, where, and a link to it; at most 10 alert emails an hour. Later repeats only count.

**Limits:** `POST /errors` is public (the website calls it server-side), validated and limited to 30/min/IP; past 300 open groups no new ones are created. Resolved groups not seen for 30 days are deleted.

### 4.12 Website check (`modules/site-audits`)

Owner's choice (2026-11-10, roadmap "Free website check"): a free check of anyone's website that brings in clients for the basic website and for improvements. A visitor gives their website's address, their email and (optionally) their name on `/website-check`; the API checks the site in the background and emails them a report in their language with the score, the main problems in plain words and a link to the full report (`/website-check/<id>`), which also invites them to ask for a basic website or write to us. The team inbox gets an email per check, and each check is a lead (`projectType: web`, `company` = the host, the message a summary with the report's link) so it can be followed up; it is not acknowledged like a contact form lead (the report is the answer).

**What is checked** (each check is `pass`, `warn` or `fail`; a category's score is the share of points, a warn counting half; the score is the average of the four):

- **Speed:** time to the first byte of the page (< 0.6 s pass, < 1.5 s warn), the page's HTML weight (< 150 KB, < 500 KB), compression (gzip or Brotli), scripts in `<head>` that block the page (≤ 2, ≤ 5) and, with `PAGESPEED_API_KEY`, Google's mobile performance score (≥ 90, ≥ 50) and Largest Contentful Paint (≤ 2.5 s, ≤ 4 s).
- **Mobile:** a `viewport` with `width=device-width`, zoom not blocked (`user-scalable=no` or `maximum-scale=1`), the page's language (`<html lang>`) and an icon for phones' home screens (`apple-touch-icon`).
- **Google:** title (10–65 characters), meta description (50–160), exactly one `<h1>`, images with `alt` (none missing; ≤ 20 % warn), canonical link, not hidden from search engines (`noindex` fails), `robots.txt` and a sitemap (`/sitemap.xml` or listed in `robots.txt`), Open Graph title and image for sharing, structured data (JSON-LD).
- **Security:** HTTPS, `http://` sending to `https://`, the certificate valid with more than 14 days left, HSTS, the protective headers (`Content-Security-Policy`, `X-Content-Type-Options`, frame protection by `X-Frame-Options` or `frame-ancestors`, `Referrer-Policy`), no mixed content (`http://` scripts, styles, images or frames on an HTTPS page) and no software versions on show (`Server`, `X-Powered-By`, `<meta name=generator>`).

The texts of every check (title and advice, Spanish and English) live in the API (`site-audit-texts.ts`), so the email and the report page say the same.

**Safety:** only `http`/`https` on ports 80 and 443, no credentials in the URL; every connection's address is resolved by the API and refused when it is private, loopback, link-local, shared, multicast or reserved (IPv4 and IPv6), so the check can't reach the server's own network; the resolved address is the one connected to (no second lookup). At most 5 redirects, each one checked again; 15 s per request, the page read up to 3 MB. Requests say `Codeqlick-WebsiteCheck/1.0 (+https://codeqlick.com/website-check)`.

**Limits:** `POST /site-audits` is limited to 3/min/IP and 5 checks a day per email; the same email and host within 24 hours returns the earlier check instead of a new one. Checks run one at a time; a check that can't reach the site ends `FAILED` and the visitor is emailed that we couldn't open it (with the reason in plain words). The report page shows no email or name.

### 4.13 Codeqlick Cloud (`modules/cloud`)

Owner's choice (2026-11-12): developer tools in the spirit of AWS, Vercel or GitHub, built in phases from what the platform already runs. **Codeqlick Cloud** is a console at **cloud.codeqlick.com** (a web app of section 6.3, same accounts), a public HTTP API under `/v1/cloud`, and a command-line tool (`codeqlick-cli/`, the `codeqlick` command). Roadmap rows "Cloud: …" in Notion; decisions 32–36.

**Phases.** Each phase ships on its own and only starts when the one before works in production.

| Phase | Services | Runs code from users? | Needs |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Console, teams, API tokens and CLI login; **static hosting** (deployments, previews, rollbacks); custom domains | No | The hosting domain (`CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN`, decision 33) and its wildcard certificate (8.3) |
| 1b | The platform's existing tools opened to developers: forms for static sites (`site-forms`), uptime and cron heartbeats with public status pages (`monitoring`), the website check as an API and a GitHub Action (`site-audits`) | No | Phase 1 |
| 2 | Builds from Git (specified below, decisions 40–42 and 51); build notifications, web analytics and a key-value store (decisions 43–45); plans and billing through Stripe, managed PostgreSQL and S3-compatible object storage (decisions 46–48, for now on the production VPS) | Yes (builds) | Stripe live (decision 20); a build host (for now the production VPS, every build sandboxed by gVisor; a separate server once builds are frequent, decision 41); storage servers or OVH Object Storage |
| 3 | Containers and apps (long-running processes, like Heroku or Render) | Yes | Several servers with microVM isolation and someone on call |

Git hosting itself is left to GitHub (or a self-hosted Forgejo): developers already keep their code there, and phase 2 connects to it. Phases 2 and 3 are only outlined here; each gets its full spec before its code.

#### Teams and projects

- **Team:** what owns projects. `name`, `slug` (3–30, unique; the console's URLs), a `plan` (only `FREE` until phase 2). Anyone signed in creates a team, accepting the Cloud terms (`/terms-of-use` on the console, versioned like Swipe's: the member row stores the version and when). A person belongs to at most 10 teams and owns at most 3.
- **Members and roles:** `OWNER` (everything, including deleting the team and transferring ownership; at least one), `ADMIN` (members, invitations and projects), `MEMBER` (deploys, domains and reading everything). Up to 10 members per team. Invitations go by email (link valid 7 days, `cloud.codeqlick.com/{locale}/invite?token=…`, the token stored as SHA-256) and are accepted by a signed-in account **with that email** (forwarded links don't work for someone else), accepting the terms. Owners and admins remove members; anyone leaves a team (not its last owner).
- **Project:** one deployable thing. `name`, `slug` (3–40 lowercase letters, digits and single dashes, unique across Cloud: it is the project's address `<slug>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>`; reserved: `www`, `api`, `app`, `admin`, `cloud`, `console`, `docs`, `status`, `mail`, `cdn`, `static`, `assets`, `codeqlick` and the web apps' names), `productionDeploymentId?`, `suspendedAt?` / `suspendedReason?`. Up to 10 per team. Deleting a project takes its sites down and deletes its deployments and domains at once; the row stays 30 days (`deletedAt`) so nobody else can take its address while old links still point at it, then the slug is free.
- **Events:** every change in a team is recorded (`CloudEvent`: who, through which token if any, `action` such as `project.created`, `deployment.created`, `deployment.promoted`, `domain.added`, `member.invited`, `token.created`, a label, details and the IP) and listed newest first in the console. Kept 365 days. Admin actions on Cloud also go to the platform's activity log.

#### Authentication for machines

- **API tokens:** `cqk_` + 32 random bytes (base64url). Shown once; only the SHA-256 and the first 12 characters (to recognise it) are stored. A token belongs to the person who made it and acts as them, with their role in each team, optionally **restricted to one team**. Expiry 30, 90 or 365 days or never; the console shows when and from where (IP) it was last used. Tokens deploy and manage projects, deployments and domains; members, invitations, tokens and teams themselves need a signed-in session. Up to 20 tokens per person. Revoking deletes it. Tokens only open `/v1/cloud/*`: anywhere else they get `401`. Deactivating an account makes its tokens useless at once (checked on every request).
- **Sending a token:** `Authorization: Bearer cqk_…`. `JwtAuthGuard` sends anything starting with `cqk_` to `CloudTokensService` (routes marked `@CloudApi()` only); the request's user then carries `via: 'token'`, `tokenId` and `tokenTeamId`.
- **CLI sign-in (device flow, like RFC 8628):** `codeqlick login` asks `POST /cloud/cli/device` for a `deviceCode` (secret, kept by the CLI) and a `userCode` (`ABCD-EFGH`, no ambiguous letters), opens `cloud.codeqlick.com/{locale}/cli?code=ABCD-EFGH` and polls `POST /cloud/cli/device/token` every 5 s. On that page the signed-in person sees the code, the machine's name (sent by the CLI) and when it was asked for, and approves or denies. Approving creates a 90-day token named "CLI · <machine>", handed to the CLI once on its next poll. Codes expire after 10 minutes and are deleted after a day. For CI, a token made in the console goes in `CODEQLICK_TOKEN` instead.

#### Static hosting

- **Deployment:** an immutable upload of a built folder (the output of `astro build`, `vite build`, `next export`, plain HTML…). `codeqlick deploy [dir]` packs the folder as `tar.gz` (dotfiles left out except `.well-known/`; `.codeqlickignore` lines with `*` wildcards left out too) and sends it to `POST /cloud/projects/:id/deployments` as multipart (`bundle` plus `target` `preview` or `production`, and optionally `message`, `commit`, `branch`, all shown in the console, and `config`, the project's `codeqlick.json`: see "Redirects, rewrites and headers"). The API checks and unpacks it into its own folder before answering:
  - only regular files and folders (links, devices and anything else refused); relative paths without `..`, each part 1–255 bytes of printable characters; at most 10,000 files, 100 MB unpacked and 50 MB uploaded;
  - an `index.html` at the root;
  - the team's storage (the sum of its kept deployments) stays under its plan's limit, and 30 deployments an hour per team.
  Anything refused answers `400` (or `413` for size, `429` for the rate) with the reason, and nothing is kept. Each deployment records its size, file count, who made it and with which token.
- **Addresses:** every deployment answers at `https://<slug>--<short id>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>` (`short id`: its first 8 hexadecimal characters) for as long as it is kept; the production deployment also at `https://<slug>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>` and the project's verified domains. A `production` deployment becomes production as soon as it is unpacked. **Promote** makes any kept deployment production (that is how a rollback works: `codeqlick rollback` promotes the one before). Switching is atomic (a link replaced by a rename), so visitors never see half a site.
- **Serving:** Nginx serves the files straight from disk (8.3): `index.html` for folders, `<path>.html` without the extension, `404.html` when it exists for anything missing, and `200.html` (when it exists) for every missing path instead, which is how single-page apps route. Assets under `/_astro/`, `/assets/` and `/_next/static/` and any file with a content hash in its name are cached for a year; HTML is revalidated. Every response carries `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin` and `Strict-Transport-Security`; HTTP goes to HTTPS. Deployment addresses (the `--` ones) answer with `X-Robots-Tag: noindex`. A `codeqlick.json` can change how paths are answered (redirects, rewrites, headers, clean URLs, trailing slashes): see below. A project without production, suspended or deleted answers a Codeqlick page "nothing here" (404) or "suspended" (451).
- **Kept deployments:** the last 20 of each project plus its production one; older ones are deleted with their files every hour (`CloudHostingService`). Deleting a deployment by hand is allowed unless it is production.
- **Limits of the free plan** (the API's `CLOUD_PLANS`, shown on the usage page): 3 teams owned, 10 projects per team, 1 GB of storage per team, 100 MB and 10,000 files per deployment, 30 deployments an hour, 5 domains per project, 10 members, 20 tokens per person, 100 redirects and rewrites and 30 header rules per deployment; and in phase 1b, 3 forms per project and 1,000 submissions a month per team, 10 monitors per team, 20 website checks an hour per team (preview checks count too). Traffic is fair use: Nginx limits each address to 50 requests a second (bursts of 100) and the team is told before anything is suspended for traffic.

#### Custom domains

A project serves its production deployment on up to 5 domains of its own (`docs.example.com`, `example.com`, `www.example.com`: each is added separately).

1. **Add:** refused when it is `CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN` or under it, `codeqlick.com` or under it, `SITES_PREVIEW_DOMAIN` or under it, a managed site's domain, or another project's (unique). One exception (decision 53): a domain that is exactly one of `CLOUD_PLATFORM_DOMAINS` is accepted when the project belongs to the team whose id is `CLOUD_PLATFORM_TEAM_ID` (the id, because anyone can register a slug) (that is how the documentation site gets `docs.codeqlick.com`). Other teams still get the refusal; subdomains of a listed domain are not covered; with either variable empty there is no exception. The domain is then verified, pointed and served like any other.
2. **Verify ownership:** a TXT record `_codeqlick.<domain>` with `cq-verify=<token>` (random per domain). Nothing is published for a domain until it is verified, so nobody can point a domain they don't own at us and claim it.
3. **Point it:** an `A` record to `SITES_SERVER_IP`, or for a subdomain a `CNAME` to `<slug>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>`. Once it resolves here, the API writes `domains/<domain>` (the project's slug inside) in `CLOUD_ROOT`; the host's `sync-sites.sh` (every minute) runs `cq-site add <domain> cloud <slug>`, which serves the project's production folder with a Let's Encrypt certificate. When it answers over HTTPS the domain is `LIVE`.

States: `PENDING` (not verified), `VERIFIED` (verified, not pointing here or not over HTTPS yet), `LIVE`, `ERROR` (it stopped resolving here, with the reason; it comes back on its own). `CloudDomainsService` checks the ones not `LIVE` every 10 minutes for 7 days (then daily) and the `LIVE` ones every 6 hours; "Check now" in the console checks one at once (10/min). Removing a domain deletes its file and `sync-sites.sh` unpublishes it.

#### Password protection

Owner's request (2026-11-13; Notion CQP-149): a project can ask visitors for a password before they see its deployments, and optionally its production too, so work in progress can be shown to a client without being public. Decision 50.

- **Setting** (on `CloudProject`, every plan): `protection` `OFF` (default) | `PREVIEWS` (every deployment address `<slug>--<short>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>`) | `ALL` (the previews plus production: `<slug>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>` and the project's custom domains). One password per project (8–128 characters), stored only as an Argon2id hash (`protectionHash`, like account passwords); `protectionDays` (how long access lasts once the password is entered: 1, 7 or 30; default 7); `protectionVersion` (an integer, raised whenever the password or the mode changes, which ends every access given before). Turning it on needs a password; switching between `PREVIEWS` and `ALL` keeps the password unless a new one is sent; `OFF` deletes the hash, so turning it on again needs a password again. Changing the days doesn't end access already given. The project's admins (team owners and admins) change it, from the console or the CLI; each change is a team event `project.protection_changed` with the mode, the days and whether the password changed (never the password).
- **How Nginx enforces it** (8.3), without reloading: the API keeps one empty marker file per protected kind of address in `CLOUD_ROOT/protect/`: `<slug>.previews` for `PREVIEWS` and `ALL`, `<slug>.production` for `ALL`. They are written or removed on every change, removed when the project is deleted, and checked against the database when the API starts and every hour (with the deployments' cleanup), so a lost or stale file is put right. A marker belongs to the project's slug, not to a deployment, so deploying, promoting and rolling back change nothing. On every request the hosting site works out the slug and the kind from the label (`previews` when it ends in `--<short>`, `production` otherwise; a custom domain's site is `production` with its slug written in), and makes an `auth_request` to an internal location that answers `204` at once when neither the marker nor the deployment's `.cq-rules` exists (see Redirects, rewrites and headers: this one check also carries the deployment's rules), so unprotected sites without rules never reach the API. Otherwise it asks the API (`GET /v1/cloud/edge/check`, formerly `/protection/check`, always as `GET` without the body), sending `X-Cq-Protection-Key` (`CLOUD_PROTECTION_KEY`, section 4.5), `X-Cq-Host` (the address), `X-Cq-Project` (the slug), `X-Cq-Kind` and `X-Cq-Access` (the value of the visitor's `__Host-cq_access` cookie, taken out of `Cookie` by a `map`; the site's other cookies never reach the API). The API answers `204` (let through) or `401`; Nginx answers a `401` with a `302` to `/__codeqlick/password?next=<the path and query>`. Nginx keeps each answer 60 s per address and cookie value (`proxy_cache`), so a page with many files asks the API once; a changed password or mode therefore reaches visitors already let in within a minute (removing a marker, at once).
- **The check:** `204` when the cookie is valid for that address (signature, project, the current `protectionVersion`, not expired), and also when the project isn't protected for that kind (a marker not yet removed), is suspended (its "suspended" page shows as usual) or doesn't exist; `401` otherwise. A request without the right key answers `403`, which the visitor sees as an error: protection fails closed.
- **Visitor's page:** `/__codeqlick/` on every hosting address (the hosting domain's and custom domains) is proxied by Nginx to the API's `/v1/cloud/protection/` with the same headers (and no `auth_request`), so that path of a project's own files is never served. `GET /__codeqlick/password?next=` answers a small Codeqlick page: the logo (inline SVG), the project's name, a password field and a button, in English or Spanish from `Accept-Language` (English otherwise); no external resources, `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'; img-src data:; form-action 'self'; frame-ancestors 'none'; base-uri 'none'`, `X-Robots-Tag: noindex`, `Cache-Control: no-store`. `POST /__codeqlick/password` (form fields `password`, `next`) checks the hash: right, it sets the cookie and answers `303` to `next`; wrong, the page again (`401`) with "Wrong password". `next` is used only when it is a path on the same address (starting with `/`, not `//` or `/\`), else `/`; Nginx can't encode a query, so it sends `$request_uri` as is and the API takes everything after the first `next=` as the path. Tries are limited to 5 a minute per IP (IPv6 by its /64) and project, and 60 a minute per project from everyone, in the API (in memory; over it the page says to wait a minute, `429`) and to 10 requests a minute per IP on `/__codeqlick/password` in Nginx (`limit_req`).
- **Cookie:** `__Host-cq_access` (host-only, `Secure`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, `Path=/`, `Max-Age` = `protectionDays`). Its value is `v1.<projectId>.<protectionVersion>.<expiry>.<signature>`, the signature an HMAC-SHA256 of the rest and the address, with a key derived from `CREDENTIALS_SECRET` with HKDF-SHA256 (salt `codeqlick`, purpose `cloud-protection-v1`, as `SecretBox` derives its keys). It is valid only on the address it was set on: the hosting domain is shared by every team, so nothing is ever set for the parent domain (decision 33), and a visitor of an `ALL` project enters the password once per address. Changing the password or the mode (a new `protectionVersion`) or `CREDENTIALS_SECRET` ends every cookie.
- **Team members skip it:** where the console links to a protected address (a deployment's, production's or a custom domain's), the link goes to a console route that asks `GET /v1/cloud/projects/:id/protection/ticket?host=<address>` (any member) and redirects to its `url`, `https://<address>/__codeqlick/enter?ticket=…`. The ticket is signed like the cookie (purpose `cloud-protection-ticket-v1`), names the project, the address and a random id, lasts 60 s and works once (its id kept hashed in memory until it expires, like the meetings' tickets). `/__codeqlick/enter` checks it, sets the same cookie and answers `303` to `/` with `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`; an expired, used or foreign ticket shows the password page with "This link has expired". A member's access then lasts `protectionDays` like anyone's. The CLI's printed addresses stay plain.
- **What it doesn't change:** forms, analytics, the key-value store's public reads and status pages are on the API's or the console's host, not the hosting site, so they work as before (a form's `Origin` is still the project's address). Monitors and the website check (`codeqlick audit`) of a protected address see the password page, as any visitor would.
- **Console:** the project's **Settings** tab gets a "Password protection" card: the mode (off, previews, everything), the password (write-only: empty keeps the current one; required when turning it on), the access days (1, 7, 30) and Save; ADMIN only, members see the state. Protected projects show a lock next to their addresses.
- **CLI** (`codeqlick` 0.5; the project linked in the folder, or `--project`): `codeqlick protect` prints the state; `codeqlick protect previews|all [--days 1|7|30]` asks for the password without echo in a terminal, twice (or reads standard input when it isn't a terminal, like `env add`); an empty answer keeps the current password when there is one (to change only the mode or the days). `codeqlick protect off`. `--json` prints the API's answer. `codeqlick deploy` and `builds` print, on standard error, that the address asks for the password, so `--url-only` stays one line.
- **Privacy:** no IP is kept (the tries' limit lives in memory); the cookie holds no personal data and is set only after the visitor enters the password, so it is strictly necessary and needs no consent banner.

#### Redirects, rewrites and headers (`codeqlick.json`)

Owner's request (2026-11-13; Notion CQP-148): a deployment declares its redirects, rewrites and response headers in a `codeqlick.json`, as on Vercel or Netlify, without touching Nginx. Decision 52.

- **One file, two uses.** `codeqlick.json` is already the CLI's project link in the project's folder (`{ team, project, dir }`, section 4.13 CLI). The same file gains the keys below; the link keys stay and the server ignores them (as `$schema`). Unknown keys are refused, so a typo (`redirect`) is an error, not a silent no-op.

```json
{
  "$schema": "https://api.codeqlick.com/v1/cloud/schema/codeqlick.json",
  "team": "acme", "project": "web", "dir": "dist",
  "cleanUrls": true,
  "trailingSlash": false,
  "redirects": [{ "source": "/old/:slug", "destination": "/blog/:slug", "status": 301 }],
  "rewrites": [{ "source": "/app/*", "destination": "/app/index.html" }],
  "headers": [{ "source": "/*", "headers": { "Content-Security-Policy": "default-src 'self'" } }]
}
```

- **Where the server reads it.** The deployment upload (`POST /cloud/projects/:id/deployments`) takes an optional multipart field `config`: the file's JSON text. `codeqlick deploy` sends the `codeqlick.json` of the folder it runs in (not of `dir`); the build agent sends the one in the build's root folder (the project's `rootDir`, the source, not the output). When `config` isn't sent, a `codeqlick.json` at the root of the bundle is used (so a file copied into the output folder by a framework's `public/` works). `config` wins when both exist. The unpacked `codeqlick.json` is always deleted from the deployment's folder, so `/codeqlick.json` answers `404` (neither the link keys nor the rules are public). A file with none of the rule keys stores nothing (`config` null).
- **`$schema`.** `GET /v1/cloud/schema/codeqlick.json` (public, `application/schema+json`, CORS for any origin, cached a day) is the JSON Schema of the file, made from the same Zod schema the API validates with (`z.toJSONSchema`), so editors autocomplete it. It carries the hard maximums, not the plan's limits.
- **The CLI keeps the file whole.** `writeProjectFile` (`codeqlick link`, and the prompt of `deploy`) reads the file, sets only `team`, `project` and `dir`, and writes everything else back as it was (including `$schema`, the order of keys and unknown keys), so linking never drops rules. A file that isn't valid JSON is never overwritten: the CLI stops and says so.

**Rules.**

| Key | Value |
|---|---|
| `cleanUrls` | `true` (default): `/about` serves `about.html`, as today. `false`: only exact files and `<folder>/index.html`; `/about` doesn't serve `about.html`. The `.html` address keeps answering either way |
| `trailingSlash` | unset (default): `/about` and `/about/` both answer as today, and a folder asked for without its slash (only `<path>/index.html` matches) gets a `301` to `<path>/`, query kept, as Nginx does without rules. `true`: a path without a file extension in its last segment gets a `308` to the same path with a slash (`/about` → `/about/`). `false`: the reverse (`/about/` → `/about`). Never applied to `/`, to paths whose last segment has an extension, or to paths no file or rule answers. The query is kept |
| `redirects` | `[{ source, destination, status? }]`; `status` 301, 302, 307 or 308 (default 308). `destination`: a path, or an absolute `https://` or `http://` URL. The request's query string is kept unless the destination has its own |
| `rewrites` | `[{ source, destination }]`: serves another file of the deployment and leaves the URL as it is. `destination` is a path inside the deployment, with no query and no host: Cloud never proxies or fetches another address. It is resolved to a file (the exact file, then `<destination>/index.html`, then `<destination>.html` when `cleanUrls`), so a destination such as `/app` serves `/app/index.html`; when none exists the rewrite answers nothing (`/.cq-none`) |
| `headers` | `[{ source, headers: { name: value } }]`: headers added to the responses of the paths that match |

- **Patterns** (`source`; compared with the request's decoded path, without query; case-sensitive): start with `/` and contain no `?` or `#` (a source is a path: the query is never matched); segments separated by `/`; `:name` matches one segment (not empty, no `/`); `:name*` and `*` match the rest of the path, possibly empty (`/docs/*` also matches `/docs`; `*` is `:splat`). A destination may use the same `:name` and `:splat`, only those the source defines. At most 5 parameters, 500 characters per source, 2,000 per destination. A destination path starts with a single `/` (`//host` and anything but `/`, `https://` and `http://` is refused). A source starting with `/__codeqlick/` or `/__cq_` is refused (Cloud's own paths). A rule whose source equals its destination is refused (a certain loop).
- **Order** for one request, evaluated by the API (below): (1) the `redirects`, in file order, the **first** match wins, before any file is looked at (like Vercel: a redirect can hide a file); (2) `trailingSlash`; (3) the file system: the exact file, `<path>.html` (when `cleanUrls`), `<path>/index.html`; (4) only when no file matched, the `rewrites` in file order, the first match wins (like Netlify's non-forced rules: a file always beats a rewrite); (5) otherwise the usual fallback (`200.html`, `404.html`). The `headers` apply to every response served from files or rewrites (not to redirects, which carry only `Location`): **every** matching rule adds its headers, in file order, and for the same name the later rule replaces the earlier.
- **Allowed headers.** Nginx can only add headers it knows by name, so the list is fixed; matching is case-insensitive and the name is sent as written here: `Content-Security-Policy`, `Content-Security-Policy-Report-Only`, `Cache-Control`, `Permissions-Policy`, `X-Frame-Options`, `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy`, `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy`, `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy`, `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, `Access-Control-Allow-Methods`, `Access-Control-Allow-Headers`, `Link`, `X-Robots-Tag` and `Content-Language`. Anything else is refused with the reason: those Cloud sets itself and a deployment can't weaken (`Strict-Transport-Security`, `X-Content-Type-Options`, `Referrer-Policy`), those that would break the response or identify the site (`Set-Cookie`, `Content-Type`, `Content-Length`, `Content-Encoding`, `Location`, `Host`) and every other name ("not an allowed header; allowed: …"). `X-Robots-Tag` is added next to ours, so deployment addresses stay `noindex` whatever the file says. A rule's `Cache-Control` replaces the default one of that path (the year for hashed assets, revalidation for the rest). Values: printable ASCII, no CR or LF, up to 2 KB each; up to 16 KB of values per rule; at most 20 headers per rule.
- **Limits** (`CLOUD_PLANS`: `configRules`, `configHeaderRules`, `configBytes`): free 100 redirects plus rewrites, 30 header rules and a 64 KB file per deployment; PRO 2,000, 200 and 256 KB. A file over its plan's size is refused before it is parsed.
- **Validation.** The API parses the JSON and checks it with Zod when it unpacks the deployment, before keeping anything. An invalid or over-limit file answers `400` naming the exact path of the first problem (`redirects[3].status: must be 301, 302, 307 or 308`; `headers[0].headers.Strict-Transport-Security: not an allowed header`; `redirects: 101 rules, the free plan allows 100`; invalid JSON: `codeqlick.json: invalid JSON at line 4`), with `errors: [{ path, message }]` (up to 20) in the body, and nothing is kept, like every other unpack refusal. A build whose `codeqlick.json` is refused fails with that message as its reason and in its log. Unpacking also refuses any entry whose name begins with `.cq-` (reserved for Cloud's own markers).
- **Storage.** The parsed rules (without the link keys) are kept with the deployment (`CloudDeployment.config`, null when none), and the API writes an empty marker `.cq-rules` in the deployment's folder when the file has any rule or sets `cleanUrls: false` or `trailingSlash`. Uploads never create it (the line above), so it can't be faked. The rules belong to the deployment, so a rollback or a promote needs no work: the production link points at another folder, with its own marker. The hourly cleanup rewrites a marker the database says should exist and the folder lacks.
- **How Nginx applies it** (8.3), without reloading, through the same single `auth_request` as password protection (a location can have only one; decision 52). The internal check location answers `204` at once when neither `CLOUD_ROOT/protect/<slug>.<kind>` nor `$realpath_root/.cq-rules` exists, so sites with neither never reach the API. Otherwise it asks `GET /v1/cloud/edge/check` (bodiless, no visitor headers) with the protection headers (4.13 Password protection) plus `X-Cq-Deployment` (the id, taken from `$realpath_root` by a `map`), `X-Cq-Uri` (the main request's `$uri`, kept in `$cq_uri` by a `set` at server level, because the subrequest's own `$uri` is `/__cq_check`) and `X-Cq-Args` (`$args` the same way). The API decides everything: it checks the password cookie first (`401` as before), then, when the deployment has rules (read from its stored `config`, compiled once and kept in memory: deployments are immutable), evaluates them with its own read-only `stat` of `CLOUD_ROOT/deployments/<id>` (so Nginx sends no file flags). It answers `403` with `X-Cq-Status` and `X-Cq-Location` for a redirect (`Location` is built whole, query kept as above, percent-encoded), or `204` with `X-Cq-Path` and `X-Cq-H-<name>` for each header to add. `X-Cq-Path` is the file to serve: the exact file, `<path>.html` or `<path>/index.html` found by the order above, or the rewrite's destination; when no file answers (a `cleanUrls: false` path, a request for `/.cq-*`, a path nothing matches) it is `/.cq-none`, which no file can match, so the `200.html` and `404.html` fallbacks apply as usual. A deployment without rules gets an empty `X-Cq-Path` and today's behaviour. Nginx copies the answer with `auth_request_set` (`$cq_status`, `$cq_location`, `$cq_path_raw`, one `$cq_h_<name>` per allowed header) and acts in the main request: `error_page 403 = @cq_redirect` returns the redirect (four `if ($cq_status = 30x) { return 30x $cq_location; }` lines; anything else, such as a wrong key, falls to `return 403`), and the content locations use `try_files $cq_path $cq_path.html $cq_path/ /200.html =404`, where `map $cq_path_raw $cq_path { "" $uri; default $cq_path_raw; }` makes an empty answer today's list (`$uri $uri.html $uri/ …`) and a rule answer exact. Headers are added with `add_header <Name> $cq_h_<name> always;` for each name of the allowed list (an empty variable adds nothing). `add_header` isn't inherited by a location that sets its own, so those lines are in a file included at server level and in every location that repeats the site's security headers (the hashed-assets one; its `Cache-Control` default is a `map` that is empty when a rule sets `Cache-Control`). Nginx caches each answer 60 s (`proxy_cache`, key `$host|$cq_access` as before for protection alone; with rules `$host|$cq_access|$cq_deploy|$request_uri`), so each URL costs one API call a minute at most. If the API is down, protected or rules-enabled sites answer `500` for answers not cached (protection already fails closed); other sites are unaffected.
- **Console:** the deployment's page shows "Rules: N redirects, N rewrites, N header rules" (plus `cleanUrls` and `trailingSlash` when set) and the parsed file, read-only; the list shows a small "rules" badge. Nothing is edited in the console: the file travels with the code.
- **CLI** (`codeqlick` 0.5.0): `codeqlick deploy` sends the folder's `codeqlick.json` as `config` and prints the API's validation errors, one per line with their path (exit `1`). `codeqlick config check` validates the local file without deploying: it sends it to `POST /v1/cloud/config/check` 🔑 (the CLI has no dependencies, so no local validation against the schema, and the answer always matches the server's limits for the team) and prints the same errors or "ok: N redirects, N rewrites, N header rules"; `--json` prints the API's answer.
- **Privacy:** nothing new is kept; the API sees the path and query of requests whose answer isn't cached yet (the same as Nginx's access log, not stored by the API).

#### Abuse and moderation

Hosting other people's files makes us responsible for answering complaints (DSA). The Cloud terms forbid phishing, malware, illegal content, content that infringes others' rights, sexual content, spam landing pages and cryptocurrency mining in visitors' browsers. Every hosted page can be reported at `cloud.codeqlick.com/{locale}/report` (the address, a reason, details and the reporter's email; public, 3/min/IP, honeypot): the team inbox gets an email and Admin → Cloud lists the report. An admin **suspends** a project with a reason: its addresses answer "suspended" at once, deploying is refused (`403`), and the team's owners are emailed the reason with the way to reply; lifting it restores production. Admin → Cloud also lists teams and projects with their usage.

#### Privacy

Static hosting stores the files developers upload (theirs, under their responsibility) and Nginx's access log of each visit (IP, address, user agent), kept 14 days on the server for security and abuse, as for every site we host; the console says so and the Cloud terms name Codeqlick as the processor of those logs for the team. The account export (`/auth/me/export`) adds the person's teams, tokens (names, never the secret) and events they caused. Deleting an account is refused while it is the last owner of a team with other members (transfer it first); otherwise its own teams and their projects are deleted with it.

#### Forms (phase 1b)

A project's static pages post their forms to Codeqlick Cloud, which keeps each submission, emails it and shows it in the console. No backend of their own needed.

- **Form:** `CloudForm` of a project (up to 3 on the free plan): a `name`, a public `key` (`cqf_` + 16 random bytes; in the page's HTML, so it can be replaced), `enabled`, `notify` (emails each submission or not) and `recipients`: the team members who get them (1–5, by account; never a typed address, so a form can't be used to send email to strangers). Replacing the key stops copies of the old one.
- **Endpoint:** `POST /v1/cloud/forms/:key`, public, `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, `multipart/form-data` without files, or JSON. Any fields: up to 30, names up to 60 characters, values up to 5,000, 20 KB in all (files are refused with `400`). Fields starting with `_` are instructions, not data: `_redirect` (where to send the visitor afterwards), `_gotcha` (honeypot: a filled one is answered as a success and kept nowhere), `_subject` (the email's subject). An `email` field that is a valid address becomes the email's reply-to.
- **Origins:** a browser post (`Origin` header) is accepted only from the project's addresses: `https://<slug>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>`, its deployments' `https://<slug>--<id>.<…>` and its verified domains; others get `403`. Posts without `Origin` (curl, servers) are accepted.
- **Answer:** JSON callers get `{ ok }` (`200`, or `400` with `{ ok: false, error }`); a plain form is sent (`303`) to `_redirect` or back to the page it came from, when either is on the project's addresses, with `?form=ok|error`; otherwise a short "sent" page in Spanish and English. CORS lets the project's origins call it with `fetch`.
- **Limits:** 10 posts a minute per IP (across forms); the team's plan sets submissions a month (free: 1,000, counted per Madrid calendar month; over it posts get `429` and the owners one email that month).
- **Submissions:** `CloudFormSubmission`: the fields (JSON), the `email` when there is one, the page it came from (`Referer`, origin and path only) and when; no IP is kept. Listed newest first in the console, searchable, deleted one by one, exported as CSV (`GET …/submissions.csv`, UTF-8 with BOM, a column per field seen). Kept 180 days, then deleted (`CloudFormsService`, daily).
- **Emails:** each submission to the recipients in their language: the form and project, the fields as label/value rows, reply-to the visitor's `email`, a link to it in the console.
- **Privacy:** the team decides what its forms ask and is the controller of those data; Codeqlick keeps them as its processor (the Cloud terms say so, version `2026-11-13`).

#### Monitors and status pages (phase 1b)

- **Monitor:** `CloudMonitor` of a team (free plan: 10), optionally tied to one of its projects, with a `name` and one of two kinds:
  - **HTTP:** a URL (`http`/`https`, ports 80 and 443, no credentials) requested every 5 minutes with the website check's safe fetching (section 4.12: every address resolved by us and refused when private, loopback or reserved; at most 5 redirects, each checked): up when it answers below `400` within 15 s. Its certificate's expiry is read at most hourly; under 14 days left warns once per certificate.
  - **Heartbeat:** a URL of ours, `https://api.codeqlick.com/v1/cloud/heartbeats/<key>` (`cqh_` + 16 random bytes; `GET` or `POST`, public, 30 a minute per key) that a scheduled job calls when it ends. It has a `period` (1 minute to 7 days) and a `grace` (1 minute to 1 day): late when no call arrived in period + grace since the last one (or since it was created). A call with `?status=fail` (or `POST` with `{"status":"fail"}`) counts as a failure at once.
- **State:** `UNKNOWN` until the first result, `UP`, `DOWN` (HTTP: two failed checks in a row; heartbeat: late or a failure call), `PAUSED` (switched off). Every change of state is a `CloudMonitorEvent` (state, when, the error), the incident history; checks (`CloudMonitorCheck`: ok, status code, response time, error) are kept 90 days.
- **Alerts:** going `DOWN` and coming back `UP` email the monitor's chosen members (team members, 1–10) in their language, with the error and how long it was down; certificate warnings too. Never more than one alert per monitor every 15 minutes in each direction.
- **Engine:** `CloudMonitorsService` (API process, every minute, off in tests): HTTP monitors whose last check is 5 minutes old, 10 at a time; heartbeats past their deadline. An HTTP check never runs twice at once (claimed by setting `lastCheckedAt` first).
- **Status page:** one per team, `cloud.codeqlick.com/{locale}/status/<team>` (indexable when enabled): its `title`, an optional `intro`, and the monitors the team chose in its order, each with a public name (never its URL), current state, uptime over 90 days with a bar per Madrid day (the share of time it was not `DOWN`, from its events; time `UNKNOWN` or `PAUSED` doesn't count) and the incidents of the last 14 days. The overall state is "all systems operational", "partial outage" (some down) or "major outage" (all down). Also `GET /v1/cloud/status/<team>` (public JSON, cached 60 s) and `GET /v1/cloud/status/<team>/badge.svg` (a small badge with the overall state, cached 60 s). A team without an enabled page answers `404`. Status pages on the team's own domain are left for later.

#### Website check API (phase 1b)

- `POST /v1/cloud/audits` 🔑 `{ url, locale? }` runs the free website check (section 4.12, the same checks, texts and safety, PageSpeed when `PAGESPEED_API_KEY` is set) for a team's member and answers when it finishes (within 60 s, else `504`): `{ id, url, finalUrl, score, categories: [{ key, name, score, checks: [{ id, status, title, advice }] }], createdAt }`. It is a developer tool: no lead, no email. 20 an hour per team (plan `auditsPerHour`); a URL that can't be opened answers `422` with the reason.
- **Preview checks:** a project can have the same check run on each pull request's preview ("Check each preview", specified under Builds from Git; decision 52). It uses the same code and safety as `POST /v1/cloud/audits`, but no member runs it: the audit is stored with `userId` null, no `audit.run` team event is recorded (the build is the trail) and the report's language is English. It counts in the team's `auditsPerHour` like any other check. Preview addresses answer `X-Robots-Tag: noindex` (Static hosting), which the check would always fail as "hidden from search engines", so for a preview check the `indexable` check is left out of the Google category and the score.
- `CloudAudit` keeps each one (the team, who, the URL, score and result) 30 days; `GET /v1/cloud/teams/:team/audits` lists them (each with `preview: true` when it was made for a build, `userId` null), `GET /v1/cloud/audits/:id` returns one. The console's "Website check" page runs one and shows the recent ones, the preview ones marked "Preview check".
- **In CI:** `codeqlick audit <url> [--min-score 80] [--fail-on fail|warn] [--json]` prints the score and the problems and exits `1` below the minimum or when a check of that level appears, so a GitHub Actions step (`npx @codeqlick/cli audit https://<preview> --min-score 80` with `CODEQLICK_TOKEN`) fails the pull request. `codeqlick deploy` prints the deployment's URL on its own line with `--url-only`, to chain them.

#### Builds from Git (phase 2)

Owner's choice (2026-11-12). A project connected to a GitHub repository is built and deployed on every push, and its pull requests get preview addresses. Decisions 40–42.

- **GitHub App "Codeqlick Cloud"** (one, owned by the Codeqlick organisation, installable by any account): permissions *Contents: read*, *Metadata: read*, *Pull requests: read and write*, *Commit statuses: read and write*; events `push`, `pull_request`, `installation`, `installation_repositories`; webhook `https://api.codeqlick.com/v1/cloud/github/webhook` with a secret; setup URL `https://cloud.codeqlick.com/github/setup` with **Request user authorization (OAuth) during installation** on. Settings in `GITHUB_APP_*` (8.4); without them the Git tab says it isn't set up and nothing else changes.
- **Connecting:** in a project's **Git** tab an admin presses "Connect GitHub" and installs the app on the repositories they choose (`https://github.com/apps/<slug>/installations/new?state=…`; the state is signed, names the team and the person and lasts 30 minutes). GitHub comes back to `/github/setup?installation_id&code&state`; the API exchanges `code` for the person's GitHub token, checks with it that the person can see that installation (`GET /user/installations`), and only then links the installation to the team (`CloudGitInstallation`: its id, the account's login and type). The token isn't kept. An installation belongs to one team. Then the admin picks the repository (the installation's repositories, through an installation token) and the production branch.
- **Build settings** (project, all optional): root folder, install command (`npm ci`), build command (`npm run build`), output folder (`dist`), Node version (`20`, `22` or `24`), build other branches as previews (on), build pull requests (on), **check each preview** (`previewChecks`, off: it costs website checks and time; see Back on GitHub).
- **Environment variables** (`CloudEnvVar`): up to 50 per project, names `[A-Z_][A-Z0-9_]{0,63}`, values up to 4 KB, each for production, previews or both. Values are sealed (AES-256-GCM, key from `CREDENTIALS_SECRET`, purpose `cloud-env-v1`) and are write-only: the console shows names, never values; only the build that needs them receives them, and the build log hides them.
- **What triggers a build** (the webhook, signature `X-Hub-Signature-256` checked, deliveries deduplicated by `X-GitHub-Delivery` for a day): a push to the production branch (a **production** build); a push to another branch (a **preview**, when on); a pull request opened, reopened or updated from a branch of the same repository (a preview with its number; **pull requests from forks are never built**: their code is a stranger's). Branch deletions, tags and commits whose message contains `[skip cloud]` are ignored. A newer build for the same project and branch cancels the queued ones before it. "Deploy again" in the console builds the branch's latest commit. A deploy hook (a secret URL a CMS can call) does the same for its branch.
- **Build** (`CloudBuild`): `QUEUED` → `RUNNING` → `SUCCEEDED` (with its deployment) or `FAILED` (with the reason), or `CANCELED`. One running build per team at a time; at most 15 minutes; the free plan builds 200 times a month per team (Madrid calendar month; over it builds are refused with a note in the console and a failed commit status). The log (stdout and stderr, at most 1 MB: the end is kept) is shown live in the console and kept with the build 30 days; builds are kept 90 days.
- **Build server** (decision 41): a build host runs `codeqlick-builder` (section 8.4; for now the production VPS itself, with every build sandboxed by gVisor), an agent that **asks** the API for work (`POST /v1/cloud/builder/claim`, every 5 s; it needs no open port). For each build the API hands it a one-hour installation token for that repository only, the commit, the settings and the variables. The agent clones the commit, runs install and build in a **throwaway Docker container** (`node:<version>-bookworm-slim` under gVisor, the agent's own unprivileged user, 2 CPUs at low priority, 2 GB of memory, 512 processes, internet only: no access to the host, its private networks or Docker; removed afterwards), sends the log every 2 seconds and a heartbeat every 30, and finally uploads the output folder as a `tar.gz`, which the API deploys exactly like `codeqlick deploy` (same checks and limits, decision 34), together with the `codeqlick.json` of the build's root folder as `config` when it has one. A running build without a heartbeat for 2 minutes is failed ("the build server stopped answering").
- **Dependency cache** (roadmap item proposed by the agent team, 2026-11-14; decision 51): builds keep the package managers' download stores between runs, so a build doesn't download every dependency again.
  - **Two caches per project.** `production` is used by builds whose `target` is `production` (the project's production branch, `CloudProject.gitBranch`); `preview` by every other build (other branches and pull requests). A build reads and writes only its own cache, so a preview build never writes what production builds install: code from a branch or a pull request can't poison the production cache. Caches are never shared between projects or teams.
  - **What is cached:** the stores only, never `node_modules` or the build output: npm (`npm_config_cache=/cache/npm`, with its logs sent to `/tmp` by `npm_config_logs_dir` so they are not kept), Yarn 1 (`YARN_CACHE_FOLDER=/cache/yarn`), Yarn 2+ (`YARN_GLOBAL_FOLDER=/cache/yarn-berry`) and pnpm (`npm_config_store_dir` and `pnpm_config_store_dir`, both `/cache/pnpm`). These are set with `-e`, which Docker applies over the project's own variables (`--env-file`), so a project can't point the cache elsewhere. They are content-addressed, so a changed lockfile still reuses every package that matches, and the cache's key doesn't depend on the lockfile. The cache is also per Node major version (a store built under one Node may not suit another).
  - **Key:** the API computes it at claim time as `<projectId>/<production|preview>-node<major>` (`major` is the project's `nodeVersion`: `20`, `22` or `24`), for example `3f9c…/production-node22`. It matches `[a-z0-9-]+/[a-z0-9-]+`.
  - **Without cache:** `POST /cloud/projects/:id/builds` accepts `noCache` (`true`). The build is stored with `noCache: true` and the console shows it. The claim then carries `cache: { key, fresh: true }`: the agent deletes the stored cache before the install (`Dependency cache: cleared for this build`), so the build starts empty and its fresh cache replaces the old one for the builds after it. Builds started by a push or a pull request always use the cache. The console's "New build" form has a "Without cache" checkbox and a failed build's page offers "Retry without cache" (a new build of the branch's latest commit, like "Deploy again", with `noCache`); the CLI has `codeqlick builds start --no-cache`. It is the way out of a cache that has gone bad.
  - **Log:** the agent writes `Dependency cache: restored (<size>)` or `Dependency cache: empty` before the install, or `Dependency cache: off for this build` when the claim has no cache; after the build, `Dependency cache: saved (<size>)`. With `fresh` it says `Dependency cache: cleared for this build`. When the limits below delete the cache it says so (`Dependency cache: over the project's limit (<size>), deleted; the next build starts empty`).
  - **Limits** (the agent's, section 8.4; the same for FREE and PRO, no plan difference): after each build that used a cache the agent measures it; above `BUILDER_CACHE_PROJECT_MB` (default 1024) it deletes that cache. Then, while the whole cache folder is above `BUILDER_CACHE_TOTAL_MB` (default 10240), it deletes the least recently used caches (last use is the folder's modification time, set at each use). Builds run one at a time on an agent, so nothing is locked. The agent measures only after the build's container is gone (a cancelled or timed-out build is removed with `docker rm -f` and waited for; its cache is then left as it is, not measured or saved), so build code can't swap files under it. Before measuring or deleting it makes the whole tree readable and writable for its user (`chmod -R u+rwX`, which doesn't follow symbolic links), counts the space really used (allocated blocks, at least 4 KB per file or folder, so millions of empty files count), never follows symbolic links, and deletes a cache it can't fully read. The least-recently-used pass runs after the output is uploaded, so it doesn't delay deployments, and only looks at folders whose names match the key pattern. Deleting a project deletes no cache at once: its caches go out by that same least-recently-used rule.
  - **Disk guard:** the API knows the disk's state at claim time (`CloudDiskService`, "Disk guard (one server)" below). While it is `full` builds still run, but the claim sends `cache: null` and the log says `Dependency cache: off for this build`, so nothing grows on the disk. The API's reading is that of the disk holding `CLOUD_ROOT`: it is the builder's disk only while the builder runs on the same machine; on a separate build server the size limits are what protect its disk.
- **Back on GitHub:** each build sets the commit status `Codeqlick Cloud` (pending → success with the deployment's address, or failure with the build's page). A pull request gets one comment from the app, updated with each build: the preview's address and the build's state. With **Check each preview** on (decision 52), the comment also carries a website check of the preview:
  - **When:** a build whose trigger is a pull request (target preview) succeeds, its deployment is live and `previewChecks` is on. The API then runs the website check (section 4.13, Website check API, "Preview checks") on the deployment's own address (`https://<slug>--<short id>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>`), in the background: `finish` neither waits for it nor fails because of it, and neither do the commit status or the build notifications. Branch pushes and manual builds are not checked, nor are production builds.
  - **Limit:** it counts against the team's `auditsPerHour`. Past it the check is skipped (no audit is made).
  - **Result:** the audit is stored (no member) and the build's `auditId` set. When it ends, the API rewrites the comment with the same builder as the build's own report. Only the pull request's latest build updates it: a check that ends after a newer build was made for that pull request doesn't touch the comment.
  - **Comment section "Website check":** while running, `Website check: running…`. Done: the score and the four categories' scores (Speed, Mobile, Google, Security), up to 5 failed checks (their titles only, no advice), the number of warnings and a link to the audit in the console (`<CLOUD_URL>/en/console/<team>/audits/<id>`). Skipped: `Website check skipped: hourly limit reached`. When the preview couldn't be opened or the check ran over 60 s: `Website check: couldn't open the preview (<reason>)`, the reason being the website check's own plain-language one. The comment is in English, like the rest of it (the app has no per-team language).
  - **Only the latest build writes:** before the "running…" line and again before the result, the API checks that the build is still the pull request's latest (cancelled builds don't count); an older build writes the plain success comment and runs no check. No check runs when the GitHub App isn't configured or the repository was unlinked. A check cut short by an API restart is not retried: the comment keeps "running…" until the next push.
  - **Not checked:** Cloud has no password-protected previews, so there is nothing to skip for them. If previews get a password, the check skips them with a note in the comment (to specify then).
- **Console:** the project's **Git** tab (connection, settings, variables), its **Builds** (list with state, branch, commit, duration; a build's page with the live log, refreshed every 3 s while running; "Deploy again", "Cancel"; the "New build" form's "Without cache" checkbox and a failed build's "Retry without cache", and a build run without cache says so; for a build with a preview check, its score with a link to the audit's report). Deployments made by a build show its commit and link to it. The Git tab's "Build pull requests" is followed by a checkbox **"Check each preview"** (`previewChecks`).
- **CLI** (`codeqlick` 0.3, owner's choice 2026-11-13; the same `/v1/cloud` routes with the person's token): `codeqlick git` shows the connection and settings (with "check previews" when on; `git connect` has no flag for it, it is changed in the console or through the API); `codeqlick git connect <owner/repo> [--branch] [--root] [--install] [--build] [--output] [--node]` links a repository the team's GitHub installations can see (with none, it prints the console's Git tab to install the app: installing needs the browser); `codeqlick git disconnect`. `codeqlick env` lists the variables (names and targets, never values); `codeqlick env add <KEY> [value] [--production] [--preview]` (both when neither is given; without a value it reads standard input, or asks without echo in a terminal, so secrets stay out of the shell's history); `codeqlick env rm <KEY>`. `codeqlick builds` lists the latest builds; `codeqlick builds start [--branch] [--no-cache]` queues one (`--no-cache` runs it without the dependency cache) and, like `codeqlick logs [number]`, follows its log until it ends (polling `?after=` every 2 s), printing the deployment's address; both exit `1` when the build fails or is canceled, so CI can wait on a build. `codeqlick builds cancel <number>`. `--json` prints the API's answer for `git`, `env` and `builds`.

#### Build notifications (phase 2)

Owner's choice (2026-11-13). A project tells people when its builds end, so nobody has to watch the console. Decision 43.

- **Channel** (`CloudNotifyChannel`, up to 5 per project, managed by the team's admins): a `name`, `enabled`, a `kind` and what it is told about:
  - **Email:** team members (1–10, like a monitor's alerts), each in their language.
  - **Slack:** an incoming webhook (`https://hooks.slack.com/services/…`).
  - **Discord:** a channel webhook (`https://discord.com/api/webhooks/…` or `discordapp.com`).
  - **Webhook:** any `https` URL of the team's (port 443, no credentials). Each delivery is a `POST` of JSON `{ event, project: { slug, name }, build: { number, target, branch, commit, commitMessage, author, pullRequest, status, error, durationMs, url }, deployment: { url } | null, sentAt }` with `X-Codeqlick-Event` and `X-Codeqlick-Signature: sha256=<HMAC-SHA256 of the body with the channel's secret>`; the secret (`cqn_` + 32 hex) is shown once, when the channel is created.
  - **Language:** Slack, Discord and webhook messages use the channel's `locale` (`en` or `es`, the console's language when it was created); emails use each person's.
  - **Events:** a build **failed** (on by default), a build **succeeded** (off by default; with the deployment's address), and whether **previews** count too (off: only production builds). Canceled builds tell nobody. A build refused because the month's builds ran out counts as failed.
- **Secrets:** Slack, Discord and webhook URLs carry their own credential, so they are sealed like environment variables (AES-256-GCM, key from `CREDENTIALS_SECRET`, purpose `cloud-notify-v1`) and write-only: the console shows the host and the last 4 characters. Changing the URL means entering it again.
- **Delivery:** when a build ends, every enabled channel that wants that event is sent one message in the background (never delaying the build or GitHub's status): URLs through the website check's safe fetching (public addresses only, checked on connection; no redirects; 10 s), a `2xx` is success. The channel keeps its last delivery (`lastSentAt`, `lastOk`, `lastError`), shown in the console. Ten failed deliveries in a row switch the channel off (`enabled: false`) and email the team's admins once. "Send a test" sends a sample event at once and answers with the result.
- **Messages:** short and in the project's words: "❌ *web* · build #12 failed on `main` (`a1b2c3d` Fix header): The build exited with code 1" with a link to the build; "✅ *web* · `main` published: https://web.codeqlick.app" with links to the site and the build. Slack gets `text` (mrkdwn), Discord an embed (red or green), email the branded layout with a button to the build.
- **Console:** the project's **Notifications** tab (list with kind, events and last delivery; add, edit, switch off, test, delete). The **Builds** tab's button says "Build <branch>" while the project has no builds yet.

#### Deploy hooks (phase 2)

Owner's request (2026-11-13; roadmap row CQP-150). A deploy hook is a secret URL that builds one branch of a project when something calls it, so a headless CMS (or any service that can call a webhook) rebuilds the site when its content changes and no commit is pushed. Decision 51.

- **Hook** (`CloudDeployHook` of a project): a `name` (1–60, unique in the project), a `branch` (always set: the production branch when none is given; it must exist in the repository when the hook is made, else `400`) and a secret **token**: `cqd_` + 32 random bytes (base64url). The token is part of the hook's URL, `<the API's public address>/v1/cloud/hooks/<token>` (`https://api.codeqlick.com/v1/cloud/hooks/cqd_…`), shown **once**, when the hook is made; only its SHA-256 and its first 12 characters (`prefix`, to recognise it) are stored, like API tokens. The hook also keeps who made it (`createdById`, null once that account is gone), when it was made and when it was last called (`lastCalledAt`).
- **Who and how many:** only projects connected to a repository have hooks (`409` otherwise). A project's admins make and delete them; members list them (session or API token). Up to 5 per project on every plan (`CLOUD_PLANS.*.deployHooksPerProject`; past it `409`). A hook can't be edited: to change its name or branch, make a new one and delete the old one.
- **Revoking:** deleting a hook revokes it at once (its URL answers `404` from then on). Disconnecting the repository, connecting the project to a different repository, or deleting the project deletes its hooks. Changing the production branch leaves them as they are: each keeps building its own branch (a hook made for the old production branch now builds previews of it).
- **Endpoint:** `POST /v1/cloud/hooks/:token`, public (the token is the credential); `POST` only. It needs no body: any body is ignored (read only up to the API's default size limit, `413` above), so a CMS's payload never matters. A call queues a build of the latest commit of the hook's branch **exactly like "Deploy again"** (`CloudBuildsService`: the branch's head read from GitHub, the month's builds counted, older queued builds of that branch canceled, the commit status and notifications as for any build). The build is **production** when the branch is the project's production branch, else a **preview**, whatever the "build other branches as previews" switch says: a hook is an explicit request. It answers `202 { build: { number, status, url } }`, `url` being the build's page in the console.
- **Refusals:** an unknown or deleted token `404`; a deleted project `404`; a suspended project `403`; a project no longer connected, or a branch that no longer exists in the repository (`404` from GitHub) `409` with the reason; GitHub failing `502`; the GitHub App not set up `503`. When the team's builds for the month have run out the call is handled like a push: the build is recorded as failed with the note "The team reached its N builds this month" (failed commit status and notifications included, nothing is built) and the call answers `429` with that reason. Every build a call queues counts against the plan's monthly builds (`buildsPerMonth`, Madrid calendar month, shared with pushes and "Deploy again") unless it is canceled: a build replaced by a newer one before it started no longer counts.
- **Rate limit:** 10 calls a minute per hook and 60 a minute per IP across hooks (both `429`, counted in the API's memory like the other public endpoints). Bursts within the limit cost little: each new build cancels the queued ones before it for the same branch, so a CMS saving ten times in a row builds once or twice (and only the builds that are not superseded count for the month).
- **The build:** `CloudBuild.trigger` is `HOOK` (`hook` in the API) and the build keeps the hook's name at that moment (`hookName`), so it stays readable after the hook is deleted; it is shown in the console's build list and page as "Deploy hook · <name>". It has no `createdById`; its commit, message and author are the branch head's, as for "Deploy again".
- **Events:** every call that finds a hook of a live project is a team event `deploy_hook.called`, whatever the answer (`202`, `403`, `409`, the month's limit): label the hook's name; details `{ status, build? }` (the HTTP status and the build's number when one was queued); the caller's IP. It has **no person**: `CloudEvent.actorId` is already optional, so it is written like Cloud's other automatic events (`CloudTeamsService.recordSystem`, which takes the caller's IP as an optional last argument for this) with `actorName` `Deploy hook <name>` (cut to 120) and no token. The console's events list shows it like any other. Calls refused before a hook is known (unknown token `404`) or by the rate limit (`429`) record nothing, so a flood can't fill the list. Making and deleting a hook are `deploy_hook.created` and `deploy_hook.deleted`, by the person (and token) that did it, with the hook's name and branch.
- **Keeping the token out of logs:** the API's request log and error log write a hook's path as `/v1/cloud/hooks/cqd_<first 8>…`, never the full token. Nginx's access log of the API writes the hook's path the same way, with only the first 8 characters of the token after `cqd_`.
- **Management API:** `GET`, `POST` and `DELETE /v1/cloud/projects/:id/hooks…` (section 5), with a session or an API token. `lastCalledAt` is only written when a call finds the hook.
- **Console:** the project's **Git** tab gets a **Deploy hooks** card: the list (name, branch, `prefix…`, made by, last called), "Add" with a name and a branch (the production branch preselected), the new URL shown once with a copy button and a warning that it won't be shown again, and "Delete" (with a confirmation saying the URL stops working at once). Without a repository the card says to connect one first. The Builds list marks hook builds.
