  `site.ts` plus its images; the demos in `examples/` are also the presets of managed sites:
  `peluqueria`, `restaurante`, `fisioterapia`, `portfolio`, `despacho` (professional office),
  `comercio` (shop), `reformas` (trades) and `general`, "Another kind of website", a neutral design
  with generic photos for any other business. `sections` orders the middle sections and `navLabels`
  renames them in the header and above their titles, e.g. a portfolio's gallery first as
  "Trabajos"). Its CI
  (`.github/workflows/sites.yml`) checks and builds the template and every demo, and builds a preset
  through the builder.
- **site-builder** (`codeqlick-sites/builder/server.mjs`, a service in both compose files, internal
  only): `POST /build` with `Authorization: Bearer $SITE_BUILDER_TOKEN` and `{ slug, preset,
  domains, content }` writes the content to a work folder, copies its images (`media/<name>` from
  the API's uploads, mounted read-only; other names from the preset), runs `astro build`, copies
  the result to `releases/<timestamp>`, swaps `current` (a relative link), keeps 5 releases and
  writes `domains` (and drops a `remove` mark). One build at a time; answers `{ ok, release, ms }`, or `422 { ok: false, error }`
  with the end of the build log. `GET /presets` returns the demos' content.
- **Domains:** `deploy/sites/sync-sites.sh` (the deploy user's cron, every minute) runs
  `cq-site add` for each domain in `sites/*/domains` that points at the server and isn't served
  over HTTPS yet. A client's own domain is theirs (decision 15): they register and pay it, and
  either make the team's OVH account its technical contact (the API then points it here and reads
  its expiry) or point `@` and `www` at the server themselves. The portal tells them which.
  Their email is theirs too: a domain newly bought at OVH includes a free Zimbra Starter mailbox
  in their account. The portal tells them to keep that option, to create `hola@<domain>` in the
  OVH manager, how to read it (webmail, IMAP/SMTP `ssl0.ovh.net`) and offers to put that address on
  the site. Zone changes never touch MX or TXT records.
- **Request previews** (decision 16): `POST /site-requests` reserves a slug (`<business label>-<6
  random chars>`) and, after storing the lead, builds the preset with the request applied in the
  background (`SitePreviewsService`) with `demo: true`, `form: { demo: true }` and `preview: true`
  (the template then puts `noindex` on every page and a bar "Vista previa preparada por Codeqlick ·
  aún no está publicada"), at `<slug>.<SITES_PREVIEW_DOMAIN>`. Until the host publishes it (about a
  minute) that address answers with another site of the server, so in production the API waits for
  it to answer 200 over HTTPS (up to 10 minutes, else `FAILED`) before marking it `READY` and
  emailing the requester the link. The "sent" page (`?preview=<slug>`) links
  `/basic-website/request/preview/<slug>`, which asks `GET /site-requests/previews/:slug`, refreshes
  itself every 10 s while it is `BUILDING` and sends the visitor to the preview once `READY` (and
  says it isn't available when it failed or was removed). At most 10 a day across all
  requests (more still become leads, without preview). They are taken down after 30 days, or once
  the lead is converted (onboarding does it at once) or marked as spam (a job every 6 hours), and
  from the lead page: the builder's `DELETE /sites/:slug` writes `sites/<slug>/remove`, and
  `sync-sites.sh` runs `cq-site remove` for each of its domains and deletes the folder.
- **Preview follow-up:** three days after a preview became `READY`, a lead still `NEW` (nobody has
  answered it), not converted and whose preview is still up gets one email in its language: "what
  do you think of it?", the preview's button and an invitation to reply with what to change or to
  book a call. It is sent once (`previewFollowUpAt`), and the lead's `followUpOn` becomes that day
  when it had none, so the board flags it for the team. The lead page shows when it went out.
- **Proposal from a request:** "Preparar propuesta" on a lead with a `siteRequest` drafts, in the
  lead's language, a proposal for its client account (prepared unregistered when missing, no email):
  title "Web de <business>", a summary with the business, kind and city, the chosen style, the
  domain (theirs or to register), their wishes and what the basic website includes; one item "Web
  básica de una página" at 200 €; terms with the two rounds of changes, the domain paid by the
  client and the optional maintenance; valid 30 days. The team reviews and sends it as any draft.
- **Monthly website report:** on the 1st (from 09:00 Madrid time) every managed site whose domain is
  `LIVE` gets last month's report, emailed to the active people of the client account with
  `monthlyReport` (each in their language): visitors and page views (Umami, with the month before
  to compare), the top 3 sources, the messages sent through its form and the uptime of its monitor,
  plus a button to the project in the portal. When Umami doesn't answer it waits for the next hourly
  check, and from the 3rd it goes out without visits. `reportedMonth` keeps it to one a month.
  Clients turn it off in their account and admins on the user page; the project page has "send now".
- **Prospects:** "Nueva vista previa" (Admin → Vistas previas) builds the preview of a business
  that hasn't asked yet, to show it in person or send it: the request's fields (style, business,
  contact details, hours, services, about, wishes) and, optionally, the person's name and email.
  It is a lead added by the team (`createdById`): never emailed automatically (no "your preview is
  ready", no follow-up), its legal email is the business's, the person's or `MAIL_FROM`, and it
  can't be converted or get a proposal until it has an email. From the lead page the team edits any
  request (form or prospect) and rebuilds its preview, rebuilds a removed or failed one, or deletes
  the lead. Previews are listed with their state, address and lead.
- **Taking a managed site down** (Admin → project → website → "Dar de baja la web", typing its
  address to confirm): see `DELETE /admin/projects/:id/site`. DNS records set at OVH are left as
  they are.
- **Website editor** (`/[locale]/portal/projects/[id]/site/editor`, decision 18): clients (and
  admins) change everything of their site: design (the style it starts from, colours with a
  contrast check, fonts, corners), the business (name, tagline, description for Google, kind of
  business, price range), the hero (texts, photo, buttons, facts), every section (shown or hidden,
  its title and menu label, its order) with its items and photos, the banner, contact details,
  hours, social links, the contact section and the legal data. Forms on the left, the draft's
  preview on the right (on phones, a "Ver" tab). Every change is saved as the draft about a second
  after typing stops; texts and colours change in the preview at once (the draft build marks its
  texts with `data-edit` paths and listens to `postMessage`), anything else (photos, sections,
  order, fonts, style) rebuilds the preview, which takes a few seconds. "Publicar" puts it online;
  "Descartar cambios" goes back to the published site. The draft's preview is a build with
  `draft: true` (noindex, links disabled, no form, no statistics) under the base path
  `/site-draft/<key>`, kept by the builder apart from the published releases, served sandboxed
  (`Content-Security-Policy: sandbox allow-scripts`). The "Tu web" page keeps the status, the
  domain and the review kit, and opens the editor.
- **Google review kit:** the site content's `contact.reviewUrl` is the business's "write a review"
  link on Google (`https://g.page/r/…/review`, `https://search.google.com/local/writereview?placeid=…`
  or a `maps.app.goo.gl`/`goo.gl` short link; other hosts are refused). Clients set it in "Tu web"
  (reviews section), with how to get it from their Google Business Profile ("Pedir
  reseñas"). With it the site's reviews section ends with "Déjanos tu opinión en Google", and the
  portal page `/portal/projects/[id]/site/reviews` shows the kit: the QR code (SVG drawn on the
  server), the link to copy and send by WhatsApp or email, and a printable A5 poster ("¿Te ha
  gustado? Déjanos tu opinión en Google", business name, QR, short steps) printed or saved as PDF
  from the browser. Without the link the page explains how to get it. The project page links to it.
- **Menu or catalogue with QR** (a paid extra, owner's choice 2026-11-10): the site content's
  `menu` is `{ title, intro?, categories: [{ name, items: [{ name, description?, price?,
  allergens? }] }], note? }` (1–20 categories of 1–60 items; `allergens` from the 14 the EU
  requires restaurants to declare: `gluten`, `crustaceans`, `eggs`, `fish`, `peanuts`, `soybeans`,
  `milk`, `nuts`, `celery`, `mustard`, `sesame`, `sulphites`, `lupin`, `molluscs`). The team turns it
  on per site (`ManagedSite.menuEnabled`, Admin → project → website, "Carta"); it is billed apart,
  like any extra. Turned off, builds leave `menu` out (the content keeps it, so turning it back on
  restores it) and the website editor's "Carta" part shows it locked with "Pídela a Codeqlick",
  a link to a new support request asking for it. Turned on, the client edits it there (shown or
  hidden, title, intro, categories and their items, allergens as checkboxes, a closing note; texts
  change live in the preview) and the template publishes it on its own page, `/carta`: the
  categories with a jump list, every item with its price and allergens (in words, never icons
  alone) and the allergen note required in Spain ("Si tienes alguna alergia o intolerancia,
  pregúntanos"). The header links to it as "Carta" (or the section's title when shorter than 20
  characters) and so does the home page, after the hero. The portal page
  `/portal/projects/[id]/site/menu` gives the QR to `<site address>/carta` (SVG drawn on the server,
  as the review kit's) and a printable A5 poster ("Escanea para ver la carta", business name, QR,
  address) printed or saved as PDF from the browser; "Tu web" links to it once the menu is on.
- **News** (included in every basic website, decision 55): the client posts
  news or offers from the portal, and fresh content helps the site on Google. It is off by default:
  the client switches it on in the website editor's "Noticias" tab (`ManagedSite.newsEnabled`;
  admins can too). Whether it should become a paid extra like the menu is open for the owner; the
  switch would then be set by the team, a one-line change. Turned off, builds leave the news out
  and the posts are kept, so turning it back on restores them.
  - **Posts are not part of the site content.** Unlike texts they have no draft/publish cycle of
    decision 18: posting news must not publish the rest of a half-edited site. Each is a
    `SiteNewsPost` (section 4.2): title, summary, body, optional photo, publication state and an
    optional expiry for offers. The photo is uploaded with the site's other photos
    (`POST /portal/projects/:id/site/images`, same rules and library). Up to 200 posts per site.
  - **Body:** plain text in a small Markdown subset: paragraphs (blank line), `**bold**`,
    `*italic*`, `[text](https://…)` links and `-` lists. A single newline inside a paragraph is a
    line break. The template renders it at build time
    with its own small renderer: everything is escaped first, no HTML or images are allowed, links
    must be `http`, `https`, `mailto` or `tel` (a link with any other scheme stays as the literal text typed) and external ones get
    `rel="noopener nofollow ugc"`. The editor shows a preview of the text as it will look.
  - **Publishing.** Publishing a post, or editing, unpublishing or deleting a published one,
    rebuilds the live site at once through the builder, the same path as publishing the site, and
    leaves the site's draft content alone. The build gets the published, non-expired posts
    (newest first) as `news: { posts }` next to `url` and `form`, and the builder copies their
    photos as it does the content's. Each post is `{ slug, title, summary, body, photo?: { file:
    'media/<name>', alt }, publishedAt, updatedAt, expiresAt? }` (dates as ISO 8601 instants, `alt`
    the title, `body` the raw Markdown); `news` is left out while the section is off or has no
    published post. Drafts and expired posts are never sent to the builder.
    A static build takes seconds, so a build per change is enough: the API runs one build per
    site at a time and folds changes made meanwhile into one more build. Saving a draft post or
    editing an unpublished one builds nothing. A failed build keeps the previous release online,
    as always (`lastBuildError`); the post stays saved and the next build includes it. Preview
    builds of the editor's draft also receive the published posts, so the menu link and the home
    section show up in it.
  - **Expiry.** `expiresAt` is a Madrid date chosen by the client (stored as the end of that day):
    the post is shown through that day and hidden after it, by the hourly job of section 4.6. The
    portal shows it as `EXPIRED` and the client can extend it or unpublish it.
  - **On the site** (Spanish, the template's language; dates formatted `es-ES`; addresses have no trailing slash, the template uses `trailingSlash: 'never'`): `/noticias`,
    the list newest first, 10 a page with `/noticias/2`…, each with its photo, date, title and
    summary; `/noticias/<slug>`, the post with its date, photo, body and a link back to the list,
    with its own title, description (the summary), canonical URL and Open Graph tags, and an
    `Article` JSON-LD (`headline`, `datePublished`, `dateModified`, `image`, `author` and
    `publisher` the business); `/noticias/rss.xml`, the feed of the latest 20 (linked from the
    pages' `<head>`); a "Novedades" section on the home page, after the hero (and the menu teaser
    when there is one), with the latest 3 and a link to all, only when there is at least one; the
    header's menu link "Noticias" (like "Carta"), and the sitemap, which lists every post and list
    page. With no published post, the news pages are not built and the link and section are left
    out, even when the switch is on. The page is an ordinary page of the site: it has the
    notices bar, the contact details and the cookieless statistics, and the draft preview marks
    its texts as everything else.
  - **Portal:** the website editor gets a "Noticias" tab (the editor's forms stay on the left): the
    switch, with a line saying what it does; the list of posts (title, state `Borrador` /
    `Publicada` / `Caducada`, dates), newest first; a form to create or edit one (title, summary,
    body with a preview, photo, expiry date) saved as a draft with "Publicar", "Despublicar" and
    "Eliminar" (with a confirmation). Posts are saved with their own button, not by the editor's
    autosave, which is the site's draft. Admin → project → website shows the same list and
    can edit, publish and delete any post, and switch the section on and off.
  - **Activity and email.** The activity log records `site_news.switched`, `site_news.created`,
    `.updated`, `.published`, `.unpublished` and `.deleted` (`targetLabel` the post's title,
    `details` with the project). Nobody is emailed, not the client and not the team: it is the
    client's own content and they see the result at once.
  - **Limits:** writes are limited like the editor's other portal writes (60 an hour), and publish
    and unpublish, which build, like publishing the site (30 an hour).
- **Form messages in the portal:** the site's form messages are kept (`SiteFormMessage`) and the
  portal page `/portal/projects/[id]/site/messages` lists them, newest first, 20 a page: name,
  email (`mailto:` link to answer), phone (`tel:` link) when given, date and the message. Each can
  be deleted (with a confirmation). "Tu web" links to it with how many it keeps. The email to the client still goes out, with a link to the page; messages are deleted
  after 12 months. The template's privacy page and the terms of sale say so.
- **Online appointments** (Citas on the basic website): the site content's `booking` is
  `{ url, label }`, the link of a Citas booking page (section 4.9;
  `<citas host>/{es|en}/<slug>`, checked against the host's `citas.` subdomain) and its button's
  text (up to 40 characters, "Pedir cita" by default). With it the template shows that button in
  the header (beside "Llamar", which becomes the secondary one; on phones it stays in the bar next
  to the menu and also heads the menu), at the top of the contact section ("Reserva online") and
  tells search engines with a schema.org `ReserveAction`. The website editor's "Citas online"
  part turns it on, picks one of the booking pages of the people of the client account (the
  editor view's `bookingPages`: title, link, whether it is paused), writes the button's text
  (changed live in the preview) and, without any page, links to Citas to create one
  (`/editar/nueva`, a new tab). A paused page keeps its link, so the button stays and Citas says
  it takes no bookings for now. The appointments themselves are Citas's: emails, reminders and
  cancelling as in section 4.9.
- **Notices** (closures and special hours): the site content's `notices` (up to 5) are short texts
  with dates, `{ text, from?, until }` (`YYYY-MM-DD`, `until` not before `from`): "Cerrado por
  vacaciones del 10 al 20 de agosto", "El 12 de octubre abrimos de 10:00 a 14:00". Each shows as a
  bar on top of every page from `from` (or at once) to the end of `until`, by the visitor's date: the
  site is static, so the bars are built `hidden` and a small inline script shows the ones in their
  window, and they go away on their own when the dates pass without a rebuild (a notice past its
  `until` stays in the content until the client removes it). Clients edit them in the website
  editor ("Avisos"), which says of each whether it is showing, scheduled or over; the draft's
  preview shows them all, and their texts change live.
- **Buying a preview** (decision 20): with `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` and `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET` set,
  the requester can buy their website from its preview for `SITE_PRICE_CENTS` plus VAT
  (`SITE_VAT_PERCENT`: 200 € + 21 % IVA, 242 €), one payment, with nobody from the team in between.
  The preview's bar adds "La quiero · 200 € + IVA" (the
  build gets `previewBuyUrl`), and so do the "your preview is ready" and follow-up emails. They lead
  to `/[locale]/basic-website/request/preview/<slug>/buy` on the website: the business, the link to
  the preview, what is included (the whole website with a contact form that works, hosting,
  the editor to change everything, choosing their domain from the client area; the domain is paid
  apart, to its registrar) and the price before VAT with the total, and a checkbox to accept the terms (`/terms`) and ask to
  start at once, knowing the right of withdrawal ends once the website is made. Its button asks
  `POST /site-requests/previews/:slug/checkout` and goes to Stripe Checkout (one line "Web básica —
  <business>" at the price before VAT with the tax rate "IVA <n> %" (exclusive; the API finds or
  creates it in Stripe once), euros, the lead's email when it has one, billing address and optional
  tax ID collected, an invoice created on payment (`invoice_creation`), the purchase in
  `client_reference_id` and the metadata). Stripe returns to
  `…/preview/<slug>/paid?session=<id>`, which follows the purchase (refreshing while it is pending)
  and then says the website is made, where it is and that an email brings them into the client
  area; a cancelled payment returns to the buy page. Stripe's webhook makes the website once it is
  paid, whatever the buyer does after paying: the lead gets the buyer's email if it had none, and
  onboarding runs from it as the team's oldest active admin (activity says it came from a purchase):
  the client account (invited to create it, decision 17), the project, the site from the request
  (slug: the business label, with a suffix when taken; no domain, chosen from the portal), its
  monitor, and the preview taken down. The buyer gets "pago recibido, tu web ya está creada" with
  its address and the client area, and the team gets an email with the purchase. If the site can't
  be made (the lead became a client meanwhile, the builder failed…) the purchase stays PAID with its
  `error`, and the team is emailed to finish it by hand. Each event is handled once. The lead page
  shows its purchases (with a link to the invoice in Stripe's dashboard), and Admin → Vistas previas
  marks bought ones. Stripe numbers and emails the invoice (the owner's tax details and "email
  finalized invoices" are set in Stripe's dashboard); the purchase keeps its id and the total
  charged. The privacy policy names Stripe as the payment processor.
- **Custom sites** keep their own repository and are uploaded with `deploy/sites/publish-site.sh`.
  The demos are published that way at `<demo>.demo.codeqlick.com`.

Details in `deploy/README.md`.

### 8.3 Codeqlick Cloud hosting

Developers' deployments (section 4.13) live on the host in `/srv/codeqlick/cloud` (mounted in the API container as `CLOUD_ROOT`, read-write and owned by its user, 1000:1000; folders 755 and files 644, so Nginx reads them):

```
cloud/
  deployments/<id>/        the unpacked files of each deployment (folders 755, files 644)
  deployments/<id>/.cq-rules  empty: the deployment has codeqlick.json rules (4.13); written by the API, never from an upload
  hosts/<slug>             link → ../deployments/<id>: the project's production
  hosts/<slug>--<short>    link → ../deployments/<id>: each kept deployment's own address
  domains/<domain>         a verified custom domain that resolves here; the project's slug inside
  protect/<slug>.previews  empty: the project's deployment addresses ask for its password (4.13)
  protect/<slug>.production  empty: its production and custom domains do too
  pages/                   the "nothing here", "page not found" and "suspended" pages (from deploy/cloud/pages)
```

- **Hosting domain:** a domain of its own (decision 33), `CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN`, with a wildcard `A` record (`*` and `@`) to the server and **one wildcard certificate** (`<domain>` and `*.<domain>`) from Let's Encrypt through the DNS challenge with OVH's API (`certbot-dns-ovh`, the keys need `GET/POST/PUT/DELETE /domain/zone/*`; renewed by certbot's timer). One Nginx site (`deploy/nginx/cloud-hosting.conf.template`) answers every `*.<domain>`: it takes the label from the host name and serves `hosts/<label>` (Nginx follows the link), so deploying, promoting and deleting never reload Nginx and never ask for a certificate. Labels that aren't `[a-z0-9-]+` and missing links answer the "nothing here" page; a suspended project's links point at `pages/suspended`.
- **Custom domains:** `deploy/sites/sync-sites.sh` (the deploy user's cron, every minute) also reads `cloud/domains/`: each domain not yet served gets `cq-site add <domain> cloud <slug>` (an Nginx site whose root is `hosts/<slug>`, with an HTTP-01 certificate), and a served one whose file is gone gets `cq-site remove`.
- **One-time setup** (root, `deploy/setup-cloud.sh <domain>`): installs `python3-certbot-dns-ovh`, writes its credentials from `/srv/codeqlick/.env` (`/etc/letsencrypt/ovh-cloud.ini`, 600), creates the DNS records through OVH, gets the wildcard certificate, installs the Nginx site and reloads; then `CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN` goes in `.env` and the next deploy creates the folders. `cq-site` must be installed again after it learns the `cloud` kind. Steps in `deploy/README.md`.
- **Password protection** (4.13, decision 50): one snippet, `deploy/nginx/cloud-protection.conf` (installed as `/etc/nginx/snippets/cq-cloud-protection.conf`), included by the hosting site and by every `cq-site … cloud` site, after each sets `$cq_slug` and `$cq_kind` (the hosting site through `map`s of the label; a custom domain's site writes its slug and `production`). It adds `auth_request /__cq_check` to the site, the internal `location = /__cq_check` (`return 204` when neither `CLOUD_ROOT/protect/$cq_slug.$cq_kind` nor `$document_root/.cq-rules` is a file, decided with a flag `$cq_need` set by two `-f` `if`s, plus an `if (-d $document_root)` that fills `$cq_dep` (the deployment id, taken from `$realpath_root` only when the folder exists, because `$realpath_root` fails a dangling link) and an `if` that answers `403` for a decoded path with control characters; otherwise `proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:4010/v1/cloud/edge/check` (API_PORT, formerly `/protection/check`) with `proxy_method GET`, no body, the `X-Cq-*` headers and `proxy_cache` 60 s for `204`, `401` and `403`, keyed by host and cookie value, or with `$cq_deploy` and `$request_uri` too when `.cq-rules` exists: set inside the location), `error_page 401` as a `302` to `/__codeqlick/password?next=$request_uri`, and `location ^~ /__codeqlick/` proxied to `/v1/cloud/protection/` (`auth_request off`, `limit_req` 10/min per IP on the password page). What belongs to Nginx's `http` level (the `map` that takes `__Host-cq_access` out of `Cookie`, the cache's `proxy_cache_path /var/cache/nginx/cq-protect`, the `limit_req_zone`) lives in the hosting site, which custom domains already need. The key comes from `/etc/nginx/cq-cloud-protection-key.conf` (root, 600: one `proxy_set_header X-Cq-Protection-Key "…";`), included by every location that calls the API. `api.codeqlick.com` answers `404` for `/v1/cloud/protection/` and `/v1/cloud/edge/`, so those routes are reached only through the hosting sites.
- **Redirects, rewrites and headers** (4.13, decision 52) add no second check: they extend the protection snippet's one `auth_request /__cq_check` (above). Added there: `set $cq_uri $uri; set $cq_args $args;` at server level (the rewrite phase of the main request, before the subrequest), `map $realpath_root $cq_deploy` (`~/deployments/([0-9a-f-]+)$`), the `auth_request_set` lines (`$cq_status`, `$cq_location`, `$cq_path_raw`, `$cq_h_<name>` for each allowed header), the `map` giving `$cq_path`, `error_page 403 = @cq_redirect` and `location @cq_redirect` (`auth_request off`), and `proxy_cache_path /var/cache/nginx/cq-protect` stays the one cache. `deploy/nginx/cloud-rule-headers.conf` (installed as `/etc/nginx/snippets/cq-cloud-rule-headers.conf`: one `add_header <Name> $cq_h_<name> always;` per allowed header, generated from the API's list so they stay in step) is included at server level and inside the hashed-assets location. The hosting site's `try_files` lines take `$cq_path` (4.13). The API reads deployments through `CLOUD_ROOT` mounted read-only for its stat (it already has it read-write). No new key or env var. Existing `cq-site … cloud` sites need `sudo cq-site add <domain> cloud <slug>` once more, as for protection.
- **Setting it up** (manual, once; steps in `deploy/README.md`): run `deploy/setup-cloud.sh <domain>` again (it creates `CLOUD_PROTECTION_KEY` in `.env` when missing, writes the key file and the snippet, renders the hosting site again and reloads), recreate the API, install the newer `cq-site` again, and run `sudo cq-site add <domain> cloud <slug>` once more for each domain in `cloud/domains/` so their sites include the snippet (the certificate is reused).
- **Limits at the edge:** `limit_req` per host (50 r/s, burst 100), uploads never reach Nginx's hosting site (they go to the API, whose `client_max_body_size` for `/v1/cloud/projects/` is 55 MB).
- **Backups:** the database dump includes Cloud's tables; deployments' files are not backed up (developers keep their sources and redeploy). The docs say so.
- **The documentation site** (section 4.13, decision 53) is hosted here like any project: a project of the Codeqlick team with Git builds, served at `codeqlick-docs.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>` and at `docs.codeqlick.com`, which `cq-site` publishes as a custom domain (`cq-site add docs.codeqlick.com cloud codeqlick-docs`) with its own certificate. Nothing in `deploy/` is special for it; the DNS record is the owner's.

### 8.4 Cloud's build server

Builds from Git (section 4.13) run other people's code (their `npm install` scripts included), so each one runs in a sandbox that keeps it away from the host, from other containers and from the platform's data.

- **Machine:** for now the **production VPS itself** (decision 41: the owner's choice until a separate machine pays off; it has 8 vCPU and 22 GB, and builds run one at a time). Moving to a dedicated VPS (2 vCPU, 4 GB, 40 GB, only SSH open) is the same setup with no code change, and stays the target once builds are frequent.
- **Sandbox:** every build container runs under **gVisor** (`runsc`, a user-space kernel: code in the container never talks to the host's kernel), on Docker's default bridge (`docker0`, which nothing else on the VPS uses), with no capabilities, `no-new-privileges`, the agent's own uid, 2 CPUs with a low CPU weight (`--cpu-shares 256`, so production wins under load), 2 GB of memory without swap and 512 processes. A firewall rule set (`deploy/builder/firewall.sh`, applied by the service before every start) drops everything from `docker0` to the host itself (its own services and the TURN server on the bridge address) and to private and link-local ranges (`10/8`, `172.16/12`, `192.168/16`, `100.64/10`, `169.254/16`), so a build reaches the internet (npm, GitHub) and nothing else. The other projects' containers sit on their own Docker networks, isolated from the default bridge.
- **Agent:** `codeqlick-builder/` (Node 24, no dependencies) as a systemd service under the user `builder` (in the `docker` group), with `CLOUD_API_URL`, `CLOUD_BUILDER_TOKEN` and `BUILDER_RUNTIME=runsc` in `/etc/codeqlick-builder.env`. It claims a build every 5 seconds, clones into `/var/lib/codeqlick-builder/<build>`, runs `docker run --rm --runtime runsc --network bridge --user <the agent's uid:gid> --cpus 2 --cpu-shares 256 --memory 2g --memory-swap 2g --pids-limit 512 --security-opt no-new-privileges --cap-drop ALL -v <work>:/work [-v <cache>:/cache -e npm_config_cache=/cache/npm -e YARN_CACHE_FOLDER=/cache/yarn -e npm_config_store_dir=/cache/pnpm] -w /work/<root> node:<version>-bookworm-slim sh -c '<install> && <build>'` with the variables, stops it at the time limit, packs the output folder and uploads it, and always deletes the work folder; it keeps only the dependency caches between builds (below). Images are pulled ahead of time and once a day. Before building, the clone's remote (with its token) is removed. Without `BUILDER_RUNTIME` the containers use Docker's default runtime (acceptable only on a dedicated machine).
- **Dependency cache** (section 4.13, decision 51): when the claim has `cache: { key }`, the agent keeps the cache in `<BUILDER_WORK_DIR>/cache/<key>` (created when missing, owned by the agent's user, which is also the container's) and mounts it read-write at `/cache`, next to `/work`, with the variables above. It refuses a key that doesn't match `[a-z0-9-]+/[a-z0-9-]+` or whose folder isn't inside `<BUILDER_WORK_DIR>/cache` (path traversal), and fails the build instead of guessing. With `fresh: true` it deletes the stored cache before mounting it. With `cache: null` nothing is mounted and the stored cache is left alone. Measuring and deleting follow 4.13's rules (after the container is gone, `chmod -R u+rwX` first, allocated blocks, no symbolic links followed); the work folder gets the same `chmod` before it is deleted. It touches the folder's modification time at each use, logs the lines of 4.13, and after each build measures the cache it used and applies the limits: `BUILDER_CACHE_PROJECT_MB` (default 1024) per cache, `BUILDER_CACHE_TOTAL_MB` (default 10240) for the whole `cache/` folder, deleting the least recently used first. Both are optional, in `/etc/codeqlick-builder.env` (`setup-builder.sh` adds them as commented lines with their defaults). The cache folder is outside every build's work folder, so the work folder's deletion never touches it.
- **Setup:** `deploy/builder/setup-builder.sh` (root) installs Git, Node 24 and gVisor (Docker too when missing), registers the `runsc` runtime with a Docker **reload** (never a restart: running containers keep running), creates the user, the agent from this repository (on the production VPS it clones `/srv/codeqlick`, so no deploy key is needed, and copies `CLOUD_BUILDER_TOKEN` from its `.env`), the firewall and the service, then checks that a sandboxed container reaches npm but not the host. Steps in `deploy/README.md`.
- **Updating:** `sudo /opt/codeqlick-builder/update.sh` pulls `main` (from `/srv/codeqlick` on the production VPS, so after a deploy) and restarts the agent.

## 9. Quality gates (CI)

Each app has its own workflow, triggered by changes in its folder:

- **Backend:** install → `prisma generate` → lint → format check → typecheck → unit tests → migrations and e2e tests against a PostgreSQL service → build.
- **Frontend:** install → lint → format check → typecheck → unit tests → build.
- **E2E:** the whole stack built from the commit, then Playwright browser tests.
- **Docs** (`.github/workflows/docs.yml`, on changes in `codeqlick-docs/**`): `npm ci` → format check → `astro check` → build → `npm run linkcheck` (every internal link and anchor resolves, every English page has its Spanish twin). Cloud builds and publishes the site itself from `main` (section 4.13); this workflow only gates pull requests.
- **Deploy:** after all of a commit's CI on `main` has passed, runs `deploy/ci-deploy.sh` on the server.

Changes that touch neither app (only `deploy/` or docs) run no CI and aren't deployed on their own.

**Dependencies (weekly).** Versions stay exact, so nothing updates on its own:

- **Dependabot** (`.github/dependabot.yml`) opens, every Monday, one grouped pull request per app (backend, frontend, `codeqlick-sites/web-basica`, and `codeqlick-docs` once it exists) with the patch and minor releases, one for GitHub Actions, and a pull request as soon as a dependency has a security fix (with "Dependabot security updates" on in the repository settings). Majors are left to people, and so are TypeScript minors and `@types/node` majors (the pins in section 2). Each pull request runs the normal CI; merging it deploys.
- **Audit** (`.github/workflows/audit.yml`) runs `npm audit --omit=dev --audit-level=high` in each app every Monday and by hand; a failed run emails the repository's owner.

## 10. Decisions log

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Two apps, no shared package | Independent deploys and versions; the API contract is the boundary |
| 2 | BFF: browser never calls the API directly | Tokens stay httpOnly, no CORS surface for the browser, simpler security |
| 3 | Zod + Standard Schema over class-validator | Native in NestJS 12, one schema gives validation, types and OpenAPI |
| 4 | Rotating refresh tokens with reuse detection | Stolen refresh tokens are detected and the session family is revoked |
| 5 | TypeScript 6.0 instead of 7.0 | TS 7's tooling API isn't stable yet; linters and test runners don't support it |
| 6 | Prisma 7.10 instead of 8.0 RC | Only stable releases in production |
| 7 | Node 24 LTS | Node 26 isn't LTS until late October 2026 |
| 8 | CSS Modules + tokens over a CSS framework | Small surface, no runtime, brand-driven design |
| 9 | Markdown content stored in PostgreSQL | Simple authoring, versionable later, no external CMS |
| 10 | Uploaded images on the API's disk, not in object storage | One VPS and few files: a Docker volume is enough and adds no service or credentials; the URL shape (`/media/<filename>`) would survive a later move |
| 11 | Managed basic websites are built on the server from content in the database | Clients edit their own site from the portal and it republishes in seconds; one shared template keeps 200 € sites maintainable. Custom sites keep their own repository (`deploy/sites/publish-site.sh`) |
| 12 | The basic-website template lives in this repository (`codeqlick-sites/web-basica`) | The builder image needs it and every managed site must build from the same version; no extra repository credentials on the server |
| 13 | The public website request takes no photos | Anonymous uploads invite abuse; photos come later through the client portal, where the person has an account |
| 14 | Domain expiry: RDAP when the registry offers it, a date typed by the team otherwise | `.es` has no public RDAP or WHOIS; most other TLDs do |
| 15 | The client is the holder of their domain and pays the registrar; Codeqlick manages it as the technical contact | The domain is the client's if they ever leave; Codeqlick resells nothing (no business registration yet); as OVH technical contact the team's API keys can set its DNS and read its expiry (also `.es`) without the client's password |
| 16 | Request previews are built at once, under the previews' domain, demo-mode and noindex, and removed after 30 days | Seeing their own site a minute after asking sells it; demo mode (form that doesn't send, no dialling) and noindex keep a page nobody reviewed harmless; a daily cap keeps Let's Encrypt's weekly certificate limit for codeqlick.com safe |
| 17 | Clients create their own account; the team only prepares what is theirs and invites them | Consent and credentials come from the person (`/register`, email confirmed, privacy accepted). A proposal, project or site the team prepares for an email hangs from an unregistered account for that email, so nothing else changes; it can't be signed in to until its owner registers (on their own or from the invitation), and team emails to it (a sent proposal) carry the invitation instead of the portal link. Weekly summaries and monthly reports skip unregistered accounts |
| 18 | Clients edit everything of their website, on a draft with its preview | They own it: design, texts, photos, sections and legal data are theirs to change, with nothing left to ask the team for. A draft keeps half-finished changes off the live site, and its preview is a real build of the template (no second renderer to keep in step): texts and colours are patched live, everything else rebuilt in seconds. Publishing tells the team what changed, and every publish keeps a revision |
| 19 | TypeScript 7.0 in the API and the website; the site template stays on 6.0 | The Go compiler type-checks in a fraction of the time. 7.0 ships no programmatic API, so the API is built with `tsc` instead of the Nest CLI (it only ran `tsc` and cleaned `dist`), and the template keeps 6.0 until `astro check` (or its successor for 7.1+) supports it. Supersedes decision 5 for the API and the website |
| 20 | Clients can buy their preview online, through Stripe Checkout, for 200 € + IVA with Stripe's invoice | The preview sells the website; paying for it there and getting it made at once, with the editor to finish it themselves, sells it to those who don't want a call. Stripe Checkout keeps card data off the platform and its webhook makes the site even if the buyer closes the page. Built off (no keys) until the owner was registered; switched on once registered and the gestor reviewed the terms and the price (200 € + 21 % IVA). Stripe issues each sale's invoice (numbering, tax breakdown, the buyer's tax ID when given), so there is no invoicing of our own |
| 21 | The video chat's signaling runs in the API; media goes browser to browser (WebRTC) with our own coturn | Reusing the API gives accounts, bans and moderation for free; a WebSocket with a single-use ticket keeps tokens out of the browser (the BFF rule's aim) without making Next.js proxy a socket. Media never touching the server keeps cost and liability low; coturn on the VPS avoids handing visitors' IPs to a third-party STUN/TURN. One API instance holds the queue in memory |
| 22 | Video chat: verified accounts only, adults only, explicit content allowed, declared age by default | Owner's decisions (2026-11-07). An account per person makes bans and reports stick. Declared age (date of birth, not stored) is the default because no age-verification provider is set up yet; Spanish and EU rules on explicit content expect real age assurance, so `VIDEOCHAT_AGE_VERIFICATION_REQUIRED` and `ageVerifiedAt` are ready for one. A report of a possible minor suspends at once |
| 23 | Invoices are made outside and uploaded as PDFs; the portal lists them with Stripe's | Invoicing (numbering, VAT, the records the law asks for) stays in the team's invoicing tool or with the gestor, which already does it right; the platform only shows each client their invoices and whether they are paid, next to the ones Stripe issued for websites bought online |
| 24 | Meetings: video meetings by link for everyone, guests without an account, a 6-person mesh | Owner's choice of the next own app (2026-11-08). It reuses the video chat's WebRTC, coturn and accounts, and gives the apps page one for everyone next to the 18+ video chat. Guests by link make sharing effortless; the owner's waiting room and remove keep strangers out. A mesh needs no media server (nothing to run or pay for, media never touches it) and works well up to about six people; a larger meeting would need an SFU |
| 25 | Web app subdomains are published by the release, through one root helper the deploy user may run with sudo | Each app used to need a manual command with sudo on the server (DNS, Nginx, certificate), typed from a phone. The helper is a root-owned copy, so the deploy user can't change what runs as root; it takes only an app name, builds the Nginx site from its own template, and never changes an existing DNS record or site. A failure never fails the release, and the next release tries again |
| 26 | Citas: booking pages for anyone, free times across all of a person's pages, video through Meetings | Owner's choice of the next own app (2026-11-08). It reuses the website's slot and iCalendar code, so bookings land in any calendar without connecting one; checking the owner's appointments on all their pages and a per-owner lock stop double bookings. Video reuses Meetings instead of asking for a Google Meet link. Calendar sync (Google/Outlook busy times) and payments are left for later |
| 27 | Swipe: 18+ photo swiping on its own subdomain, declared age, every photo reviewed before it goes live | Owner's request (2026-11-08) with a budget of 0 €, so no paid age-verification provider (owner's choice: date of birth only). Compensations: a verification photo with nickname and date approved by the team before any upload, pre-publication review of every photo with the rule "when in doubt, reject", reports of a minor, non-consent or a stolen photo hiding it at once, a public removal form answered in 24 h, perceptual-hash blocking of removed images, private storage with metadata stripped and no backups. Spanish and EU rules on explicit content expect real age assurance; a provider can be added later. The owner later listed it on `/apps` (2026-11-08) and asked for the Codeqlick logo and indexed public pages like the video chat's (2026-11-09) |
| 28 | Cloudflare Turnstile against bots on sign-up and sign-in, and disposable emails refused | Owner's choice (2026-11-08) among the anti-bot options. Turnstile is free, cookieless and mostly invisible; the secret stays in the API, which checks every token. If Cloudflare is down sign-in still works (the rate limits remain), so an outage of a third party never locks people out |
| 29 | Accounts are told apart as clients, web-app users or neither, worked out from their data | Owner's request (2026-11-08): one account serves codeqlick.com and every web app, so the admin list mixed clients with Videochat or Swipe users. A **client** is a `CLIENT` account the team prepared (unregistered), part of a company (member or with members), or with a project, proposal, support request or converted lead. An **app user** is any other `CLIENT` account that signed up from a web app (`User.signupApp`, stored from `/auth/register`'s `app`) or uses one (a Videochat or Swipe profile, a Meetings room, a Citas page). **Neither** (`none`): signed up on codeqlick.com with nothing yet. Derived rather than stored, so it follows the data: an app user who gets a proposal becomes a client and keeps their apps. Existing accounts got `signupApp` from the app they already used. The admin user list filters by kind, app and name or email; the dashboard's client count leaves app users out. Access is unchanged: everyone keeps one account for the site and the apps |
| 30 | Free website check with our own checks and, optionally, PageSpeed Insights | Owner's choice (2026-11-10). A lead tool for small businesses: it must be free to run and answer in seconds, so the API checks the page itself (headers, HTML, robots, certificate) and adds Google's mobile speed only when a free key is set. The visitor's site is fetched by our server, so every connection is checked against private addresses (no SSRF into the server's network). Each check becomes a lead, without the contact form's acknowledgement: the report is the answer |
| 31 | Managed sites keep their form messages for 12 months, for the portal | Owner's choice (2026-11-11). Messages only reached the client by email, where spam filters could lose them. Keeping them makes the platform store visitors' data on the client's behalf (as their processor), so it is limited: only managed sites, whose privacy page (the template's) says so; 12 months, enough to find an old enquiry; the client can delete any message. The email still goes out, so nothing changes for clients who answer from their inbox |
| 32 | Codeqlick Cloud: a developer platform built in phases, starting with static hosting | Owner's choice (2026-11-12): developer tools like AWS or GitHub. A studio on one VPS can't match them, so Cloud starts with what needs no user code running on our servers (static files served by Nginx, which the platform already does for client sites) and opens the tools it already has (forms, monitoring, the website check). Builds, databases and containers run other people's code or data at scale and need separate servers, billing and someone on call: each is a later phase with its own spec. Git hosting is left to GitHub |
| 33 | Developers' sites are served from a separate domain (`CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN`), never under codeqlick.com | Anyone can upload JavaScript. Under `*.codeqlick.com` it could set cookies for `codeqlick.com` (cookie tossing against the session cookies) and a phishing page would put the whole domain on blocklists. A separate domain, like `github.io` or `vercel.app`, keeps both away; it can later be submitted to the Public Suffix List |
| 34 | One wildcard certificate and host links instead of an Nginx site per deployment | A certificate per preview would hit Let's Encrypt's weekly limit per domain in days and every deploy would reload Nginx. With a DNS-challenge wildcard and `hosts/<label>` links, a deploy is a folder and an atomic rename. Custom domains still get their own site and certificate through `cq-site`, as client sites do |
| 35 | Machines use personal API tokens and a device-code sign-in, only on `/v1/cloud` | Tokens act as their person (with their roles), can be restricted to a team, expire and show their last use, and only their hash is stored. The device flow signs the CLI in through the browser, where the account's password, Turnstile and two-step verification already are, so the CLI never handles a password. Keeping tokens out of the rest of the API means a leaked CI secret can't read a client's portal |
| 36 | The CLI is a Node package with no dependencies, published as `@codeqlick/cli` | Developers already have Node; `npx @codeqlick/cli deploy` works in any CI without installing anything else. No dependencies (its own tar writer, Node's `fetch`, `zlib` and `parseArgs`) means nothing to audit or update and a tiny download. The API unpacks bundles with the `tar` package and its own checks |
| 37 | Cloud forms email only team members, and keep submissions 180 days | An endpoint anyone can post to that sends email would be a spam relay if recipients were typed addresses; choosing members (accounts with confirmed emails) closes that. Keeping submissions in the console means a lost email loses nothing; 180 days bounds what we hold as processor. No IP is stored: the per-IP limit lives in memory |
| 38 | Cloud monitors fetch through the website check's safe fetching, every 5 minutes, alerts after two failures | Anyone can enter a URL, so the request must never reach the server's own network (SSRF): `safeFetch` already resolves and refuses private addresses on every hop. Five minutes and two failures (as for client sites) avoid alerts for a blip; heartbeats cover what can't be fetched (cron jobs, backups, queues) |
| 39 | Status pages live on cloud.codeqlick.com, not on the team's domain | The hosting domain serves static files only; a status page needs live data, so it is a page of the console's host with a public JSON and a badge. Custom domains for status pages need a proxy per domain and are left for later |
| 40 | Builds from Git use a GitHub App linked through the installer's own GitHub authorization | An app (not a personal token) gets per-repository, short-lived tokens and only the permissions it needs, and its webhooks say what changed. GitHub's return address after installing can be forged, so the installation is linked only after the person's own GitHub token shows they can see it; that token is then thrown away |
| 41 | Builds are pulled by an agent and run in throwaway unprivileged containers sandboxed by gVisor, with internet but no access to the host or private networks; for now on the production VPS; forks are never built | A build runs code nobody reviewed (dependencies' install scripts included), so it must never reach the platform's data. A separate machine is the strongest wall, but until builds are frequent the owner chose the production VPS (November 2026): gVisor keeps the code off the host's kernel and a firewall keeps it off the host's services and the other projects' networks; moving to a dedicated machine needs no code change. An agent that asks for work needs no open port and holds no secret but its token. Containers are dropped after each build, without capabilities or the Docker socket, with CPU, memory, process and time limits. A fork's pull request is a stranger's code with the project's variables: not built |
| 42 | Project environment variables are sealed and write-only | They are usually API keys. Sealed with a key derived from `CREDENTIALS_SECRET`, never returned by the API or shown in the console, given only to the build that needs them and masked in its log, so a leaked dump or a curious member doesn't read them |
| 43 | Build notifications are per project channels (email, Slack, Discord, a signed webhook), delivered once in the background through the safe fetching | A failed build nobody sees is a site that silently stopped updating. Per project, because each has its own people and channels. Slack and Discord cover most teams with no app to install (their incoming webhooks); a signed webhook covers the rest. Their URLs are credentials, so they are sealed and write-only, and they go through the website check's safe fetching so nobody can point Codeqlick at a private address. One attempt and auto switch-off after ten failures keep a dead endpoint from costing anything |
| 44 | Cloud's web analytics are cookieless, first-party to Codeqlick and keep no personal data | Developers want to know what is visited without adding a third-party tracker and a cookie banner to their sites. Counting on our own API (not the access logs) leaves out bots and cached pages and works for single-page apps. A daily salt that is thrown away the day after counts people once a day without being able to follow them, and no IP or cookie is kept. It is opt-in per project because the team, not Codeqlick, decides to measure its visitors |
| 45 | Each project gets a key-value store in PostgreSQL, over HTTP, with a public prefix for browsers | Static sites and their scripts need somewhere to keep a few settings and counters without running a database. The platform's PostgreSQL already holds the rest of Cloud, with backups; small JSON values with limits per project don't need another server. Only keys the team names `public:` can be read without a token, so a site can read flags from the browser while everything else stays private |
| 46 | Cloud's PRO plan is a Stripe subscription per team, made through Checkout and managed in Stripe's customer portal | Stripe already takes the website payments (decision 20); Checkout and the portal keep cards, invoices, VAT and cancellations off the platform. The webhook only sets the team's plan from the subscription Stripe says it has, read again on each event, so it can't be fooled or confused by order. Going back to free deletes nothing: losing data for a failed card would be worse than a few resources over the limit |
| 47 | Managed PostgreSQL runs, for now, in its own PostgreSQL container on the production VPS, open on 5432 with TLS only and a role per database | The owner chose to start on the current server (November 2026). A PostgreSQL apart from the platform's keeps teams away from its data and lets it be restarted or moved alone; TLS, SCRAM and `sameuser` rules keep each role in its own database, connection and size limits keep one team from starving the others, and nothing changes for teams when it moves to a server of its own |
| 48 | Object storage is Garage on the production VPS, behind Nginx at s3.<hosting domain>, with quotas set in Garage | Garage is a small, maintained S3-compatible server meant for self-hosting, with an admin API to make buckets, keys and quotas, so the platform drives it instead of reimplementing S3. Quotas enforced by Garage itself hold even when the API is down. One node until the storage has its own servers (owner's choice, November 2026) |
| 49 | While Cloud shares the platform's only server, a disk guard stops Cloud growing when free space runs low | The platform's database, its backups and every Cloud service write to the same disk; a team filling it would stop the whole platform. Refusing new deployments, databases and buckets and freezing databases and bucket quotas below a floor is reversible and loses nothing, while a full disk can corrupt data. The server monitor's 85 % alert gives the team time before the floor is reached |
| 50 | Password-protected addresses are enforced by Nginx's `auth_request`, switched per project by marker files, with a signed cookie per address and single-use tickets for team members | Marker files (`protect/<slug>.<kind>`) turn protection on and off without reloading Nginx, like the host links (decision 34); an unprotected site is answered from the file check alone and never touches the API, so the cost falls only on protected ones (and a 60 s cache keeps it to one call per visitor and minute). They belong to the project, not a deployment, so new deployments, promotions and rollbacks are protected from their first request. Checking in Nginx (not in the files) also protects every file type, not only HTML. The cookie is per address because the hosting domain is shared by every team: a cookie for the parent domain would be readable and settable by anyone's site (decision 33). It carries no personal data and is signed with the project's `protectionVersion`, so changing the password ends every access with no list of sessions to keep. Members get a 60 s single-use ticket from the console instead, so the team never has to type or share the password among themselves, and nothing usable stays in a link |
| 51 | A deploy hook is a secret URL per project and branch, kept only as a hash, and its calls go through "Deploy again" and the plan's monthly builds | CMSs and similar services can only call a URL, with no way to send a header or a signature, so the credential has to be in the URL; that is why it is a long random token (32 bytes) that is shown once and stored as SHA-256, like API tokens, so a database leak reveals no working URL, and one hook per branch can be revoked alone (up to 5 per project). The URL is kept out of the logs by its prefix. A call reuses the existing path for "Deploy again" instead of a second one: the commit comes from GitHub, older queued builds of the branch are canceled (a burst of CMS saves collapses into one build), a failed commit status and the notifications work the same. Each build a call queues counts against `buildsPerMonth` (builds canceled because a newer one replaced them don't count) so a leaked URL can't build without limit, and the per-hook and per-IP rates stop a loop. The hook builds its branch even when "build other branches" is off because someone asked for it explicitly. Each call is a team event without a person (`CloudEvent.actorId` is optional), so the team sees who rebuilt the site |
| 52 | A deployment's redirects, rewrites and headers live in `codeqlick.json`, are evaluated by the API inside the one edge check Nginx already makes for password protection, and are applied by Nginx from the answer's headers, from a fixed list of headers, with no proxy rewrites | Nginx can't be reloaded per deployment (a reload for every upload would drop connections and one team's file could break everyone's, decision 34), and Nginx's own rule syntax would let a client write directives. A marker file (`.cq-rules`, like decision 50's) sends only deployments with rules to the API, which validates them with Zod when the deployment is unpacked and evaluates them with its own stat of the files; Nginx only copies the answer (a status and a location for a redirect, a path to serve and the headers to add) with `auth_request_set`, so no file test, rule syntax or second subrequest lives in Nginx, and a rollback rolls the rules back with the files. The check is shared with protection because a location can have only one `auth_request`, and one call, one cache and one key are simpler than two, and the rules need the password check first anyway. (`X-Accel-Redirect` was rejected: Nginx handles it before caching, so every request would reach the API.) Deployments without rules or protection never touch the API; the cache is 60 s, short because the protection answer can change. Headers come from an allowlist because Nginx can only add headers it knows by name and because HSTS, `nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy`, cookies and content types are ours to keep on a hosting domain shared by every team (decision 33). Rewrites only point inside the deployment, so Cloud never fetches a third party's URL for a visitor (no SSRF, no open proxy). The rules go in the file the CLI already keeps in the project's folder, so they travel with the code and one file configures the project; the CLI therefore must merge when it writes the link (rewriting the file would silently drop rules) |
| 53 | The public documentation is an Astro static site built and hosted by Cloud itself, with its own search index, and `docs.codeqlick.com` is allowed by an env-gated exception for one team | Owner's request (2026-11-13). Dogfooding: the docs go through Git builds, custom domains and static hosting, so the first people to hit a problem are us. Astro with the template's exact dependencies adds no package, and plain Markdown keeps pages easy to write and review (MDX would be a new dependency). A build-time JSON index and a small vanilla script give search with no dependency, no third-party service and no tracking of what visitors type. Domains under `codeqlick.com` are refused so nobody can host a lookalike (decision 33's reasoning); the exception is exact domains in `CLOUD_PLATFORM_DOMAINS`, usable only by the team whose id is in `CLOUD_PLATFORM_TEAM_ID`, empty by default, so it widens nothing for anyone else. Pages of unmerged features are `draft: true`, so the site never describes what doesn't run |
| 54 | Usage alerts come from a periodic sweep that claims one row per team, limit and month, and go to the team's owners at 80 % | A sweep every 10 minutes over the teams with recent activity costs one query set per active team and puts nothing on the hot paths (posting a form, recording an analytics event, deploying), which stay as cheap as they are. A unique row (`CloudUsageAlert`: team, limit, month) inserted with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` makes it idempotent: two sweeps or processes never mail twice, and the owner's request (one email per limit per month) is the table's key. Owners only, because they pay and pick the plan; members and admins see the console banner. 80 % leaves time to act (delete deployments, upgrade, wait for the 1st) and is a constant because per-team thresholds would be settings nobody asked for. The banner reads `meters` from the usage endpoint so the console and the email use the same numbers as the limits |
| 55 | News on the basic website: posts outside the draft/publish cycle, one build per change, included in every site | Fresh content helps a small business on Google, and the client should post an offer without help. Posts are not part of the site content because decision 18's draft/publish is for the site as a whole: posting news would otherwise publish whatever else the client is half-way through editing, or wait behind it. A post has its own state, and publishing it builds the live site from the published content plus the published posts, never touching the draft. A build per change is fine because the site is static and builds in seconds (one at a time per site, changes made meanwhile folded into one more), so there is no second renderer and the pages are plain files, fast and indexable. The body is a small Markdown subset rendered at build time with everything escaped, so a client can't put HTML or scripts on their site. Expired offers are hidden by an hourly rebuild instead of a script in the page, so Google never sees an expired offer as current. Included, switched on by the client (off by default), because it costs little to run and makes every site better; no emails, since it is the client's own content. **Open for the owner:** whether it should be a paid extra like the menu (section 8.2). Because the switch is a column of its own (`newsEnabled`), making it paid only means that the team sets it, as for `menuEnabled`, and the editor shows it locked with "Pídela a Codeqlick" |
| 50 | Newsletter is double opt-in with its own subscriber table, sends each post once from a background job, and unsubscribes in one click | Owner's roadmap item CQP-30 (blog readers had no way to follow besides RSS). Double opt-in is the proof of consent GDPR asks for (the confirmed date and language are kept) and stops people being signed up by others. Readers need no account, so subscribers are their own table, not `User`. One-click unsubscribe (RFC 8058 headers plus a visible link to a page with a button) is required by Gmail's and Yahoo's bulk-sender rules, and buttons (on both the confirm and the unsubscribe page) keep link scanners from confirming or unsubscribing anyone. Marking a post as sent before emailing it means a crash can lose an email but never sends one twice, which is worse; claiming it with one conditional update keeps that true with more than one API process; the migration marks every live post as sent so the first run emails nothing old. The job lives in the API process like the other background work, so no scheduler or mailing service is added; sending one by one at most 3 posts per run keeps the SMTP account's limits safe |
| 51 | Builds cache the package managers' stores per project, separately for production and previews | Every Git build installed its dependencies from scratch: slow builds and wasted bandwidth on the shared build server (Vercel and Netlify cache per project). Only the download stores are kept (npm, Yarn, pnpm), not `node_modules`: they are content-addressed, so they stay safe to reuse when the lockfile changes, and they carry no postinstall output or build leftovers. Production and previews have separate caches because preview builds run code from branches and pull requests: if they wrote what production installs, that code could poison production builds. Per Node major version, since a store built under one may not suit another. Least-recently-used caps (per cache and in total) keep the shared disk safe, and the disk guard turns the cache off while the disk is full. The agent only touches a cache once the build's container is gone and never trusts what build code left in it (permissions reset, links not followed, real blocks counted), so the caps hold against hostile builds. A build asked `noCache` starts from an empty cache that then replaces the old one, so a broken cache is never a dead end |
| 52 | Pull-request previews can be checked automatically and the result goes into the pull request's comment | Teams learn about broken SEO, accessibility or performance only after release; the website check and the app's pull-request comment already exist, and joining them gives each pull request a quality report where it is reviewed. It is a project setting, off by default, because every check costs an audit and up to a minute of server time. It counts against the team's `auditsPerHour` and is skipped past it, so it can't be used to flood the check or to fetch more than a member could. It runs in the background after the build is done, so deploys aren't slower and a failing check never fails a build. The audit has no member (it is made by the build) and the comment is in English, the language of the rest of the app's comment: Cloud teams have no language of their own. Previews are noindex by design, so that one check isn't counted |
